MeDOUGA PATENT SELF—RAISING FLOUR "CAI USED

LADIES' LETTER

DIE DEAR NELLIE,

Just now black hats are very much to the fore, and there appears to be every promise of their reign continuing. They are stylish and becoming and they lend themselves admirably to almost any coloured gown. If a little brightness is liked, some colour harmonising with the rest of the

costume may be introduced, either in the form of flowers or silk rosettes with paste buckles in the centre. For cycling, shooting and walking, felt hats boat shape, trimmed with a band, a bow and a feather or two are most suitable.

A very desirable little blouse and one most be- coming to a slight figure, as three inches of the waist portion fits quite closely, has the fulness starting above this plain part. This blouse is arranged on a fitted lining, and the serge is put on from the neck downwards in three parts, but does not reach to the waist by three inches. Tiny pleats are set in the fronts, and a few at the centre back, and when the serge fits the lining, it is stitched along twice by machine, and three plain pieces of serge, fitting the lining, are laid over the three inches of uncovered lining, and rows of braid or velvet are run along, and finished with rosettes.

In front there is a little fall vest of soft washing silk, and a sailor collar of white serge, run with blue braid, or bound with blue silk. A blue silk collar outlined with a tiny pleating also looks well, and it is merely a choice between a dark or light collar, as the make is exactly the same in either case. The collar is made on stiff union, and there should be a strip down each front, as these are loose and require to be firm. The collar looks well in silk, with lines of braid, or in serge, mad there are side pieces kept stiff, which spring from the collar, and are trimmed to correspond. The sleeves are plain, with a little extra width and depth at the top, this set in two deep tucks which almost form epaulettes. This model is also most effective for a black blouse with coloured lining, and the coloured lining may be simply sateen, using silk of the same shade for collar, front, and belt, and also for those small pieces on the neck- band.

A costume eminently desirable for early autumn wear as the coat made in covert fashion, with "fly" fastening, and can be worn open, or closed, making a straight front falling away from the figure. This costume is carried out in grey tweed, slightly flecked with stone-blue, and the coat limed with glace silk in grey shot with yellow, this turning outwards to revers. The plain skirt fastens up the left side over a separate slip, and Is faced at the opening with the glace silk, a small pJrtion being left uncovered to simulate a silk o ndation. For home morning wear a cool, dainty gown would be in pale blue and white crepe cotton, made in princesse form, but with a Watteau pleat in loose gathers at thi back, and the fronts turned back in flat, straight revers from neck. to hem, and finished With a two-inch frill of cream lace. The front of full cream sateen or crepe cotton, fastened with knots of turquoise ribbon, and &sash of this ribbon coming round the waist from the Watteau pleat. Garnitures of narrowest black ribbon velvet are much employed just now on light summer gowns, muslins, figured silks, and so on, and, if prcperly applied, they are singularly effective. A Orunty frock is of figured muslin in softly-blended old *world tints, made up over vieux-rose silk, and trimmed with black }Abe velvet, which is applied on the skirt at each side in irregular lines, broken by Louis Seize knots, and forming a tablier effect. The bodice is simply fashioned and has wavy lines of the velvet across the front, three groups of three lines, each trio terminating under a graceful knot.

Black and white and black and heliotrope remain as popular as ever, and biack toilettes relieved with white, and sparkling with jet, are completed by feather boas; the blonde indulges in some touch of pink or vivid turquoise in the chapeau, and the brunette accentuate 3 her black attire with some bright tangerine-yellow, the shade now so fashionable and yet uncommon, as only the few can wear it.

The latest collars on smart capes and a raps are of less exaggerated height, and wired to acquire that graceful outward curve. The space between the collar and the neck is filled in with extremely full pleated frills of the silk lining, these setting out like a huge ruffle. The rounded capes slope off very long at the back, and are invariably trimmed with frills, and for the middle-aged long stole ends or square fronts are placed beneath the sloped cape, which, without detracting from the fashioable style, make the mantle more protective, and veils the figure. Your loving JRNum

PLEASANT PARAGRAPBS.

*NOTHING is MOTS- discouraging than unappre• dated sarcasm. HE :"I am going to kiss you when I go." She: “Leave the house at once. "Jun' imagine the case, now, Tompkins. If you were in a burning house with your wife and your mother-in-law, and could only save one, which would it be ?" "Well," replied Tompkins, thoughtfully, "I fancy I'd save myself." THE Vierrar : "So this is really good for rheu- matism, eh?" The Chemist: "Splendid, sir. I -know a man who wasn't able to walk downstairs, and the day after he finished the second bottle he awas arrested for scorching on a bicycle in the public streets.' STILL HOPE.—" I shall never marry," declared Iliss Elderly in a tone meant to be firm. "Don't -may that," answered her beat friend. "Women elder than you have had proposals." "GIVEN AWAY."—They were talking about the 'various methods of celebrating the passing of the gild year and the coming of the new. "Did you ever dance the old year out and the new year in?" 44 Yes, indeed, scores of times," was the reply. And then she was sorry she spoke. REMINISCENT.—" If I had known how sarcastio qou were I never would have married you," he said. "You had a chance to notice it. Didn't I say 'This is so sudden' when you proposed after a two-years' courtship." His WIFE: "Oh, George, I was in hopes that you would bring a new cook with you to-night." The Suburbanite: "Well, the lady I engaged this time prefers to come down on her bicycle to- morrow." JOHNNY: "You're the meanest, hatefulest, spitefulef.. thing I know." Tommy: "An' you're the crabbedest, ugliest—" Father : "Boys, boys? You forget that your mother is in the room." "WHY are they calfed pyramids, pa?" asked 'Georgie, who was looking at a picture of those wonders of Egypt. "They are called pyramids, My sons," replied the father, without hesitation, '"because, you see, they 'pear amid the general desolation of the desert." "TELL your mother, Johnny," said his kind maiden aunt, as she placed a piece of cake in his hand, "that I was very sorry your sister couldn't come." "And what will I say," replied little Johnny, with an air of strategy, "if mamma asks me where is sister's piece of cake?" " I SUP:OSE most of the patients in your charge at this asylum are quite rational on many points?" "Oh, yes ! Some of them quarrel about the different makes of bicycles and tyres just as if they were sane." A Paws Bohemian is telling his mode of life to a friend from the provincial districts. "In the morning I awake," says he, "and ring for my valet-de-chambre." "How I you keep a valet-de- chambre ?" "Well, no, but I keep a bell." ENTHUSASTIO professor of 'physics discussing the organic kingdom) : Now, if I should shut my eyes —so—and drop my head—so—and should not move, you would say I was a clod. But I move, .1 leap, I run, then what do you call me?" Voice from the rear: "A clod-hopper I" THE " NEw Wourer.—" What does this here New Woman' talk mean, John?" "It means, Maria," replied the old farmer, "that women be a-takin' men's places. You'll find the plough where I left it; an' when you sharpen the axe you can go on cutting up that cord wood in the yard; and I'll have supper ready when you get home." THE KIND OF HORSE TO BREED.—A farmer not a hundred miles from Glasgow bought a horse. -Going home he thought a drink would refresh it, so he got a pail of water, but it would not take it. On reaching home he gave is a supply of corn, but it would not touch that either, so the farmer was heard to say, " Ye're the vera horse for me if ye only work 1" VERY LIKELY. —A Yorkshire man and a - Leicestershire man contending for the superior fertility of their respective counties, the Leicester- shire man declared that he could turn a horse into a newly-mown field, and the next morning the grass would be grown above his hoofs. "Pooh! that's nothing," cried the Yorkshire man. " You may turn a horse into a field in Yorkshire, and not be able to find him next morning." A LEADING QUESTION.-" Is your mamma at home, my dear?" "Yes, sir." "Well, run in and tell her that one of her old beaus would like to see her." "Please, sir, which one are you?" "Which one?" "I heard papa telling mamma aly,ut all of you last night. He said one of y,_.o wai hanged, and one was a forger, and one a gambler. Which one are you ? "

ENOUGH FOR THE LlON.—Very Stout Lady -(witching the lion fed): "Pears_ to me, mister, 'that ain Er a very big piece o' meat for etch an anneal." Attendant (with the most Stupendous show of politeness) : "I &pose it does seem like wilts le net to you, ma'am, but it's enough for the hon." *FARMER JONES: "Well, Willie, what did ye !lean-Ur college this year?" 'Willie (facetiously) : "Oh, I learned to smoke, and to plough up a field with my golf clubs." Farmer Jones: " Well done. To-morrer, I'll set you to work sniokih' a' few hams, au' next: week I'll let you take th' toll clubs an' plough up a 10-acre field." WANTED THE " WHOLE." A barrister was questioning a widow; the other day, about her history. "My history," she replied, 'is simplicity itai:My first was the happiness of my life, my secend,waa goodness itself, my third -- ' " Ex- cuse Me, madam," interrupted the counsel, "but really we aren't here to guess charades." HOW' SHE KNEW.—Mrs. Isolate: "Ferdinand, what do you • think! Those people who have moved in next door have neither a lawn-mower, washtub, ironing-board, rake, hoe, wheelbarrow, stepladder, sewing machine, nor garden-hose! Mr. Isolate (astonished) : "How on earth did you find it out, my dear?" Mrs. Isolate (inno- cently) : " Oh, by the merest accident ! I tried to borrow those articles." ALWAYS SAFER. —A worldly father, after the style of Lord Chesterfield, was giving good advice to his son, who was about to make his entrance into society. "And, above all, avoid flirtations; but if you must flirt or fall in love, air, be sure it is with a pretty woman. It is always safer." " Why ?" asked the young man. "Because some other fellow will be sure to be attracted, and cut you out before any harm has been done." A SAGE PEDAGOGITIC.—" Pardon me," said the visitor, after the school had been dismissed: "but I should like to know why you threshed that red- headed urchin' without investigation as being the Cause of the disturbance which occurred behind your back? You could not possibly have seen who let that mouse loose, and he was the only boy in the room who was not laughing when you looked." '" That was the reason," replied the teacher. "MARIE," he cried in desperation, "I shall wait no longer Either you must consent to become my wife at once or we will end this farce. Your re- peated delays are killing me !" "Very well, Hubert," she answered calmly, "if you take that ffound, there is but tine alternative for me." She drew off the diamoncrwhich sparkled on her finger and handed it to him. He took it sadly, and bitterly exclaimed, "Oh, false, cruel girl, to thus carelessly trample upon a heart whose every throb is for you alcne ! For this btiuble I ha..,e no further use. Let it perish, as I hope may also the love of which it is the emblem." His hand was raised to throw the ring into the grate, but she grasped it quickly. "I merely wished to say, Hubert, that I would like you to have the wedding-ring made just a trifle smaller than this." SUNSHINE AND SHADOW. —She presents a be- wildering picture of daintily-garbed femininity as she comes quickly along the hall to greet him. Her cheeks are flushed with pleasure and her eyes glow ,again in a perfect ecstasy of contentment. Yet her voice is very soft and sympathetic as she speaks: gs I hope you have managed to keep dry. I scarcely expected that you would fulfil your promise and come this evening. Such a tremendous rainfall would have deterred most men. So glad you did, though. The demand for ostrich feathers increases every week and millinery for autumn wear is mostly adorned with this particular form of ornament. Thom of us who can boast long specimens among our steres,should forthwith unearth these treasures, have them dyed to match the costume with whioh they are destined to bo worn, and lightly recurled. ' The ostrich is the largest living; bird, and is not far inferior to the eigautio extinct Moa of Australia. The plumage of the male is a glossy black, except the long plumes of the wings, and tail, which are white. The females are dark grey. In the feathers the webs are I exactly equal, on eatsti side of the shaft, an equality which so impressed the Egyptians that they made the feather an emblem of justice. The feathers may be 'either plucked,or to avoid any pain, cut with a knife,the stumps afterwards falling out. A new feather grows to its full eize in from six to eight months. The pure whites from the wings are called bloods," the next quality " prime whites," " firsts," " seconds," and so on. The tail feathers are not so valuable. " Bloods" fetch from £4O to £5O per pound weight, in the wholesave market, and feline this noire the feathers descend to as low as 59. per pound: Tim finest feathers come from Aleppo. The Institute of Journalists could not have chosen as its president-elect a more representative and uni- versally respected journalist than Sir Wemyss Reid. He has been conspicious in the front rank of jour- nalism for thirty years. For seventeen years be edited the "Leeds Mercury," leaving that paper when he became general manager of Cassell and Co. As editor of the "Speaker," he has upheld the aistrtonsetimes unoldlciaLtraditions of Liber- alism with admirable vigor and judgment Ever a fighter, he deals smashing blows, and he can take them with excellent good nature. As a raconteur be has few rivals, and, since the circulation of good stories ought to be one of the functions of the Insti- tute of Journalists, Sir Wemyss Reid's term of office should be distinguised by a precaptible rise in that tide of anecdote which refreshes the souls of all rational men.

L" " CHAPTER XIX.

UNDECEIVED. T. - It was a dull afternoon, but Jeanne's spirits felt lightened as her • thoughts followed the noble fugitive; she ardently wished that she could have spoken with him, and assured him of her complete devotion, of her determination to be true to him. She was sitting absorbed in these thoughts, when Mathurine tapped at the door, and came in, her eyes radiant with delight, her whole face full of excitement. "Does my lady know that they are come?" she said joyously. Jeanne's thoughts were so full of the Constable that she stared at the girl in terror. "What ,inean you, girl? Who has come ? " Mathurine started in her turn; she wonderea what ailed her mistress, for both she and Anne were sure there had been love-passages between their lady and the handsome young Count of Laval. "Madame has bid me name -no name," she said, dropping her eyes, "but it is our noble leader; Gaspard and the rest of the troop are with him." Jeanne rose. "Do you mean that they are actually in the inn ? " "No, madame, but they twill be here before I could count a hundred. Shall I not braid madame 's hair afresh, and will she not change her plain coif 'Mil the broidered one?" Jeanne flushed ever so little. "You forget, Mathurine, that ; am widowed, and that my attire needs no ornament.. Where is Anne? Bid her come to me." The news had fluttered Jeanne; she felt excited, and she now sent Mathurine away; she wanted time to think. She would not cherish suspicions of Laval, that would be both unkind and unjust, .for aught she knew. She had never seen a map of France, St. Feriol might be near Lyons; she wondered why she had not asked her hostess how far distant the city was. She would not suspect the Count, but for all that she meant to keep her promise; she would certainly not tell him she had seen the Constable de Bourbon. Indeed, she could net affirm that it was really he; it was therefore better not to speak of the strangers who had stayed at the inn. The,women cam,e in smiling. "My lord Count has ordered dinner to be served in the eating-room; " Anne said; "and when I told him that madame was always served in her chamber, his Excellency bade me ask madame if she will honour him with her presence. Dinner is about to be served." "Mathurine, you can say I am coming; Anne will stay with me till I am ready to go dpurn." Jeanne saw Anne pout her lips in the old sulky fashion; she therefore concluded that the girl had not tired of Gaspard's flattery. This troubled her far more than it had done during the journey, for it was possible that Anne had become aware of the presence of strangers at the Flew de Lys, although she might not suspect their quality. It would not be safe to command the girl not to speak on this subject. She foresaw that if Gaspard really was engaged in hunting the Constable he would learn from, Anne all that-Inid happened in his absence. There was only,nae way,.., and Jeanne, after some reflection,

resolved to take - "Are the soldiers going on to the village to join the others F" she asked.

"They are all gone but one and the Cont of LavaL" Anne 'could not hide a deep blush as she stood facing her Mistress. She looked eagerly at the Countess, expecting that the name would bring at least a flush to her sweet face; but Jeanne was o,alrn and pale. "You will attend me, Anne, not only now, but whenever I have occasion to see Monsieur the Count of Laval; and you will remember that he does not wish either his name or mine to be spoken publicly. I wish you to be constantly with me, both indoors and when we 'journey again. Gillonne used to order these things for me, but now I must give, my own orders; and—and, listen, child; it is not seemly that one of my women should herself to be talked to by a trooper. You un.derstand, therefore, that in future you are to avoid that man Gaspard." The toss of Anne's head 'and the defiance tbat flamed in her eyes had made her mistress, say more than she had intended. Jeanne could hardly tell why she had taken such a dislike to this man, or why she felt that he was not fit to talk with her unsophisticated women; she was too inexperienced to guess that her prohibition had greatly' increased Anne's danger. It dicrhot occur to her to remember the old saying about "fruit that is forbidden." She presently went down to the eating-room, followed. by Anne. De Retz stood at the door, impatiently waiting for her. • • "Ah, my dear lady! "—he bent and kissed both her hands—"the ,joy of seeing you bales expres- sion. Mon Dien I .1 have pined day and night for such happiness!" Jeanne smiled genially. Anne made a deep reverence in return for the ConriCs greeting. "I am glad you are coine at last,-'monsieur," Jeanne said, "for we fin& thhs !;pince very dull. Have you seen the King, Monsieur le Comte ? " He held up his hand in•ivarning. Then he said: "I have much to tell you, when we are alone. May I not send away your woman?" "All in good time." Jeanne would not yield to him; he seemed already to be assuming a right over her. "Ah, there is our meal it is a welcome sight," she said gaily, as Marietta came in with platters and trenchers. "I hope that monsieur is as hAngry,,ps -I am, and will do justice to the pi 444 Ahnl'i ordered, and was fortunate enough to secure. I assure you, monsieur, that-a change of • food is not to be had herel/4244 have to live oxi eggs ancipaiity of venison. ,Mirroi, - I I have not seen a cake 'since. I left'alonguedect" She laughed .merrily at him. "Girls ot.ray age, you know, are' supposed to exist on cakes and sweets." „

He led the way to the upper end of the• table, which reached from one end of the long dark chamber to the other. Then, going close to Jeanne: "You will at least allow your woman to sit And cat where she is? I can thee speak more freely." Jeanne bent her head • she made.a signtto Anne, and then placed herself on a rough wooden seat beside Laval. It was, after all, very pleasant to have someone to speak to again, especially so hand- some a espalier. have heard," he said, "that damsels are made of cakes and sweets, and other such dainties—, "And I, monsieur, once . read a sonnet Which said we were imade,oU4 sighs and tears, doubts and fears,' "-she said with charming archness. , "Nay, that wrongs the lovely sisterhood; 'tis rah& we who have to sigh, though manhood for- bids' tears; while doubt and fear become our per- petual portion, 'till our lady deigns to smile onus." •Jeanne could not help laughing. "I had not thought you gave way to sentiment, my lord; I fancied only women and poets indulged in such pastimes." He looked at her with passionate admiration. "Doss, not your heart, dear lady, tell you another tale ? " His voice was so- very low that Anne could not possibly overhear him. "I will not believe that so fair a form can enshrine a cruel heart. It is, I believe, your joyous freedom from love's torments that makes you so unwitting of the wounds you have dealt." His eyes were full of pathetic entreaty, and the girl found herself perplexed how to answer. It was a relief when Marlette brought, in a second lish of food, the pullet Jeanne had inentioned, larded and stuffed till it was the size of a capon. . ,

Laval caned her a choice morsel with his claaer,

and presently he pressed Jeanne to drink a ca-iiof Afalvoisie, but she refused. • "And now I wait for my answer;" he said in a low voice. "In good tooth, wounded to the death as I am, I have no power wherewith to conjure an answer; I can but wait the sovereign pleasure of my queen." "Memsieur mocks me!" Jeanne said briskly, "lie-knows-well-enough there is-but one queen- ir France, our good and holy Queen Claude. When I go to Amboise, I hope to see that noble lady; my sainted mother in her youth was one of Queen Anne's maids, and she held Princess Claude in her arms when she was but a babe. Tell me, monsieur, when shall I go to Amboise ?" He was looking ardently at her; it seemed to him that he had never before realised her extreme charm of face and figure; but, in truth, the quiet rest at St. Feriol had greatly refreshed the girl. She had travelled so constantly since she quitted Lonvedec that fatigue, as well as the shock that had wet her at Laval, had told on her appearance.

Now. as he footeu at tier loVery race with that rarely delicate skin, more often matched with blue iyes and fair locks than with such deep grey liqu d eyes as Jeanne's, and such a rich glow of auburn hair, it seemed to him that her beauty was perfect. Her charm stirred his blood; for the time he forgot her wealth. As • she talked and smiled, a tinge of , bloom shewed on her clear, colourless skin. She reminded him of a faintly-tinted blush rose; but alas t he told himself, she shared another attribute of the flower: he feared she had thorns for =A-111'07aq thuch:- "And I grieve that you should wish,for Court pastimes and pleasures ; but I must do your sovereign bidding; full well I. know, at Amboise -there will be others who will sun themselves in your smiles, and who will try to win your favour'; though, dear lady, none can offer you a truer devotion. The fervour in his voice,, the glisten in his eyes, were real, and they troubled Jeanne; it was a shock to her that all unwittingly she had won the love of this brave, handsome young soldier. . „She supposed that she would he •enrcted to give hers in return, but she felt no answering throb of affec- tion.' She had greatly overcome her prejudice agaihst Laval—unless; indeed (and she now fell ashamed of the suspicion), he was trying to capture the Constable—but she did not. want a husband. She knew she could not marry again for some / time to come; why, then, should she be : again ottered by a betrothal? She was free, :And she resolved to keep her freedom as long as she ! could._

"I have told you, my lord that my,. reason "for going to Arnboise is not because I wish for Court pleasures; Igo to see. 'my cousin, Madame de Chateaubriand.."

"A very , fair and sufficient ,teason ; the beau" tiful Countess has many demands on her time, and many -favourites; yet without question she will gladly welcome so fair a relative • I doubt, how- ever, if she can interest herself' much on your behalf."

Jeanne looked saucily at him. • "What do you mean ? " she said gaily. "I believe you men are all jealous of my lovely •Francoise ; I have been told she possesses great interest at Court." "Doubtless," he interjected. Are you jealous of her? Rolland de la Toucbe —I mean the Vicomte d'Orbec "—she spoke. more gravely—"spoke just as you did; I believe he was vexed with me for loving her." A sombre cloud darkened her companion's face, his eyes narrowed, till suddenly their cruel glitter • reminded the girl of the fear with which they' had filled her in the chapel at Longuedec. Her clear, truthful eyes betrayed her fear; he forced a smile and said carelessly: • "Ma foi, madame, the Vicomte d'Orbec is a brave soldier, but he knows more of the camp, I fancy, than he does of the Court and its polished ways." Jeanne saw a slight sneer which he had not meant to shew. "Irs.s he found his way to the Fleur de Lys?. You speak, madame% as though you had lately talked with him." Jeanne 's blood was fired; his tone of authority annoyed her. "No, monsieur; I only wish he had come to enliven my solitude; he is my oldest, my best friend." • She did not choose to tell Laval how completely Rolland had deserted her ; and as she realised that the Count of Laval was the only protector left her, for both her brave uncles had fallen at the battle of Marignano, it seemed to her that it was unwise, if not wrong, to harden herself against his evident liking for her, • "The Vicomte d'Orbec is perhaps of kin to you ? " Jean was angered to feel a faint flush kindling on her cheeks under, the keen scrutiny of those narrow dark eyes; she knew they read her 'easily. "No," she said calmly. "Monsieur d'Orbec is a noble gentleman, and my parents loved him, but I do not know when I may see him again." , Her calm baffled Laval. , • , "Madame "—he spoke formally—"l shall, with your leave, return to the subject of my hopes; I feel that I ought not to have left you so long alone, but I have not even now had leisure to tell you the result of my journey. By the way, you had, I believe, two wayfarers in the house till lately ? " He tried to put the question carelessly, bit the keen look in his eyes did not fit with his tone. The girl was startled; she had hoped she would not be called on to fulfil her promise. Her- voice was unsteady gutshe answered: "I told you a have been alone." "'ln your positions. dear lady, it would be un- seemly to converse with strangers; but," he went on quickly, "you will not deny that strangers have lodged in this inn—aye, and traitorous strangers, whom you must have noted, but about whom you must, if you please, tell me all you have observed." Vett now he did not Understand Jeanne... He He thought that, -like other women he had known, she could be' brought to heel by firm management. She looked at him a moment steadily; then she rose,' and signed to Anne to follovr her. "Your, questions, my lord, are needleks ; they displease me because they doubt the truth of my words. lam not accustomed to be doubted, and I will therefore take leave of you." He fumed silently under her look and her tone, but he admired the dignified grace with which she walked to the lower end of the chamber.

CHAPTER XX. AT ST. BONNET.

Laval tried to soften his words and to pacify his charge, whom he likened mentally to a spoilt child, but Jeanne walked haughtily from the room without heeding his excuses. Freed from the glamour which her beauty had laid on his senses, he became very angry. lie longed to have power to force the truth from someone; but the silent hostess and her deaf henchman were alike impenetrable. They were both in one tale: all the guest chambers had been given up to the lady and her attendants, except a couple which had been retained by the soldier Raoul for the use of his lordship whenever it should please him to arrive. . _

Bt. Feriol was a good-sized village, and boasted a mayor, as well as the stalwart host of the Rouge Dragon, and therefore Laval dared not bully and threaten the timid mistress of the Fleur de Lys as he surely would have threatened the hostess of a solitary wayside inn. He gathered m fro Raoul, who alme up in the evening, that communication was frequent between the small hostel and the village. Laval told himself he was losing time; he had not found any traces of the Constable in Le Pay, and his present idea was, now that he had again possession of Jeanne, to journey quickly towards the Rhone. If it were true that the fugitive meant to escape into Italy, he must cross the river, and Laval knew that the best place for doing that would be at or near Vienne. He had paced up and down the eating-room with fiery impatience after Jeanne and her woman left it, but he soon saw that if he meant to succeed in the game he was playing he must keep a cool head and bridle his fierce temper. He judged from a certain uneasiness in Jeanne's manner that she knew more than she would acknowledge, and it was, he thought, possible that if he kept her with him she might by some uncon- scious revelation help his pursuit of the Constable. Meantime, he must not alarm her. He therefore walked down to the village, and finding Gaspard playing dice with Raoul at the Rouge Dragon, he bade them have the troop in readiness to start at sunrise. _

Gaspard yawned. "Does Monsieur is Comte still intend to push on to St. Bonnet ? " he asked. Laval frowned; the man seemed to be questioning his wisdom. "Certainly! Be ready to start at sunrise; let me find you drawn up outside the village." The easy insolence of the man's tone chafed his leader. Your pardon, my lord." It is not a keg journey to St. Bonnet, and after our forced ride to-day I had looked for some short respite." "Nom de Dieu! You may look for it, but you cannot have it, till a certain fugitive you wot of is in my safe keeping; till then, repose may be hoped for, not taken." Gaspard shrugged his shoulders, and turned away with a scowl. "Curse him for a tyrant!" he muttered, and when his chief was out of hearing he let fly a volley of fierce oaths. "He is greedy enough after his own pleasures; those of other folk may go to the deuce t " "I shall be right glad to leave this accursed hole!" Raoul answered. "Pardieu ! There is neither sport nor pleasure to be had, even for money! I meet but black looks and get surly answers. To my thinking, these folks are impatient

to see our backs'. I cannot. guess their meaning."

_ Gaspard shrugged his shoulders. “If you speak sooth, you are but a dullard, friend Raoul. What does it mean, say you? It means, my gossip, that they know whom we seek, and will not help us find our game. This is the proof that I have more of strategy in me than has our master; in his place I should stay here and watch day and night, and be Pleasant and ioTial

with all; then over the wine-cup, or from ag WoMan I f)s, 'would the truth leak out. Nom do: Diu! man, there you have it. Come, what say , you? Hare at': yott:lor ' another cast • the best Oa of three stands the cost. of a flask of Malvoisie.

' She had seen the grand mountains indistinctly on • the journey from Moulins into the Auvergne, but not like this. While she gazed, they seemed to rise higher and higher, till their rose-flecked • summits pierced the saffron-tinied sky, ;still glowing with the exquisite tints of sunrise; the imaginative girl told herself that Heaven had indeed opened t) her gaze, so lovely were the cool grey shadOws and silver sheen of radiance which lit up the giant peaks before her. Her exclamation of delight brought Laval to her side.

I

"It is a fine spectacle, is it not ? But on such a chilly morning does it not freeze you to regard it? " "Oh, no! I love the mountains! I eotila look at them, for ever!" she said simply. Facing those grand, inscrutable heights, untrodden by man, it seemed to her that the dark, scheming face beside her, handsome though it was, could not fully grasP the awfulness of their truth and sublimity. , They rode on for several hours almost in silence, and then halted for breakfast. She looked around her; but she saw that her maids and Gaspard were too near for her to hazard the question she longed to ask Laval—the result of his interview with the King. She resolved to wait until they reached St. Bonnet. W hatmotigh, Hey- wood ; and Edward Williams, Conway. On arrival at the works, light r .frashments were at once served, and subsevently an inapection was made. Some iatereetine inform atien wita reference to the works was supplied to those present by Mr. C. S. Haddeck. It appeers that the Warrington Gas Works were first originated in 1820. Tee works were built by Messrs. Barlow and Bros., and they had the manage- ment entirely in their hende until the cost of censtrue,- tion could be paid, The tswn was first lighted with gas in 1821 and the price was then 154 per thoueand cubic f oet. The fleet near I Mr. HAdchek could find of thee annual make of gas was the.: referring t 1839. Tne following table shows the progress wnieh has beea made : Make in Year. millions. Price. s. 1839 13i 15 0 First record 1847 16i 4 6 Borough incorporated 1877 128 3 3 C niperetion purchased gas work; 1898 282 210 Lot record The works were constructed on the Mersey-B'r oec site, which was only given up last ve or as a manufacturing station. The Mersey-stre it works have been rebuilt several times; but in 1879 it became necessary ta make further extensions, which f arced the Corpora- tion,who took over the gas works in 1877, to select a now site. This was found at Longford, and 16 aces of land were purchased. A sd ne from the London and North-Western Railway Company was brought on to the land; and the coal-trucks are run along the retort. house on an elevated railway. The waggons are "hopper" bottom; and by reaming the trap-doors, the coal slides out of tne tucks into the coal-store. At the present time the Corperatien are making ane-ations in the retort-house, which is 74 feat wide and 190 feet long. The retort-house is r ellared from outside wall to outside wall. The retort settings comprise two benches each containing six beds of eight D retorts 22 n. by 16in. The settings are on the Kionne system, as modified by Mr. Paterson, of Cheltenham. These settings are capable of producing rather over 11. millions cubic feet of gas per ate diem. T retort-house is arranged for West's manual stoking machinery', and the Cerperation have just en- tered into a contract fir same. Eaech setting is pro- vided with a separate hydraulic main ; and any one section can be closed off at will. It is intended to pull down the old retort-b -aches, and double the new arraagemeat. The coed-on-a is vertical, and con- sists of four rows of 12in cast-iron pipes. Each contain fourteen lengths of pipe about 30 feet high. The ex- hausters are of !nankin and Cone, manufacture, and comprise two 40,000 drums driven by an engine, and two 60,000 drums and engine combined. The ter washer by C. and W. Walker, is designed to remove the tar when passing 2 million cubic ft et of gas per day. There are three scrubbers, each capable of purifying Icubic feet of gas per day. The tower scrubber is 901 t and 915., and was erected by Ashmore, Benson, Nana, a-id Co. It is fitted with Green's screens and water distributor. At present, it is used for concentrating the weak liquor from the other washers. The washer-scrubbers ame of the Kirkham, Hulett, and Chem:her and Clapham well- known makes. The purification is finished in six boxes 471 t 3in by oft, made out of two 201 t. square boxes. As at present working, one box is arranged as a catch purifier. The material for charging the boxes is lifted by chain-elevators from the ground floor, and dep esited into b mows in the boxes, and so spread over the grids. Each Flintier has four 16 inch shoots, through which the foul material is dis- charged on to the ground fib ar. The covers are raised by a winch, which is driven by belting from a steam engine. The motor is of J. and 5. Braddoek's manufacture, and is capable of passing 70,000 cubic feet per hour. The gasholders are two te eacopic ones, and when filled they coatained about 1n millions cubic feee, of gas. There are also three small gasholders at Mersey. street which contain e million cubic feet of gas when filled. These holders are filled from the Longford works; a special main and exhaust plant having been put down for this purpose. The gas is distribume from Longford through a 20 inch and a 18 inch geverner, both of J. and J. Braddock's manufacture. At the Mersey-street works they had two 12 inch and 16 inch governors, The sulphate plant consists of a Waikar'is continuous still and heater, which is capable of working up 25 to 30 tons of liquor per day. The saturator is designed for a Wilton discharger by which all the salt is raised on to the drain- ing floor. The tar plant consists of a primary still for working ten tons of green tar.. The vapours from the primary still pass through one of Ellis° a's pate-it heaters. By this arrangement, the green tar is heated, and to some extent dehydrated. The cistern for storing the oils also acts as the roof of the press-room and anthra- cene store. The visitors were greatly interested in the working of a machine known as the "lightning painter" which by means of compressed air sprinkled the paint upon ironwork in a manner quite equal to bled painting and at a greater speed. After being photographed by Mr. T. Birtles, the mem- bers left for the Lion Hoteewhere they were entertained to lunch by the members of the GAS Committee. An ex- cellent repaet was Served by the manageress, Mrs. Gil. bert,tci whiclef nil justice was done. Several toasts were afterwards proposed and duly honeured. • The toast of the Queen and the rest of the Royal Family, which was proposed by Alderman FAIRCLOOGEL who presided, was drunk with musical honours. I hope your friends will spend, a pleasant day, and leave with a favour able impression of old War rington." (Applause.) He was sure it needed no words of his in submitting that toast for their approval. (Applause). He personally was perhaps better able to judge what progress had been made, because he had I been through the old Mersey-street works as well as the ILongford works, and he was pleased to say that great I progress had been made. (Hear' hear). Of course they ,all knew that most of that work was due to the gas manager, but he ventured to assert that no gas manager in the world could make strides unless he was suppotted by the chairman and vice-chairman of his committee. That was essential to good management. The name of Alderman Fairclough was a household word in Warring- ton, and be wan one of the giants of industry in the dis- trict. Councillor Heaton was a gentleman he had bad the pleasure of knowing personally for a number of years. He had made himself a name in the Ueda of the district, and had risen from small things up t very great things. He was sure Mr. Haddock must feet very proud of having gentlemen of that description at the head of his committee. (Applause). Warrington was making progress in the matter of the productioa of gas. They had it before them that at one time gas in War- rington cost 15s per 1,000 cubic feet. (Laughter). To- day the charge was a somewhat less figure, and he thought Warrington bad, so to speak, caught the affinity of the district in being near to Widnes. (Hear, hear, and laughter). He had great pleasure in complimenting the Corporation of Warrington upon the advanced policy they were pursuing. Not long ago they made a reduc- tion in the charge, and now, in a very short time, he understood it was proposed to mike a considerable further reduction. (Applause). It showed that they were not only making gas for the benefit of the con sumers, but they were fully alive to the best interests of the undertaking. He was sure that as long as the War.

riagton gas works were in the hands of such gentlemen as he had had the honour to name, toe concern was bound to go on progressing. (Applause). It was before only possible to proeuce have a million at Longford, but during the summer they had increased the production another half million. They would be able to produce a million in the new retort house and that effected a very great saving in the cost of production, and enabled them to reduce the price 3d. They hoped with a little more work on the same lines to get down the capital charge and give the benefit to the gas consumer.(Applause.) Up to the present time the gas consumer had had to pay the piper. They hid been un- able to get any grant from Government for the cost of the transfer, and the whole cost had been defrayed out of revenue. As regarded their institution, he might say that the membership was now 140. Tneir object was to produce gas at a low cost. PROPOSED REDUCTION OF ARgAMENTS.

THE TSAR SUGGESTS A CONFERENCE.

The St. Petersburg "Official Messenger " publishes the following : " By order of the Tsar, Count Muravieff, on the 24th inst.,handed to all the foreign representatives accredited to the Court of St. Petersburg the following communi- cation The maintenance of general peace and a possible reduction of the extensive armaments which weigh upon all nations present themselves in the existing con- dition of the whole world, as the ideal towards which the endeavors of all Governments should be directed. The humanitarian and magnanimous ideas of his Majesty the Emperor, my au iist master,have lren won over to this view. In the conviction that this lofty aim is in conforrn ty with the most e!sential interests and the legitimate views of all Powers,the Imperial Govern- ment thinks that the p, erect moment would be very favourable for seeking by means of international dis- CURgiOil the moat effectual means of ensuring to all peeples the benefits of a real and durable peace, and above all of putting an end to the progressive develop- ment of the present armaments. "In the cmrse of the last twenty years I the longings . for a general appeasement have 1 grown especially pronounced in the con- science; of civilised nations. The preservation of peace has been put forward as the object of international policy, It is in its name that great States haveconcluled between themselves powerful affiances : it is the better to guarantee peace that they have developed in propor- tions hitherto unprecedented their military forces and still continue to increase them witltout shrinking from any sacrifice. All these efforts nevertheless have not yet ' been able to bring abmt the beneficent results of the d sired pacification. " 'The financial charges following an upward march strike at the public prosperity at its very source. The intellectual and physical str.ength of the nations' labour and capital are for the major part diverted from their natural application and unproducti rely consumed. hundreds of millions are devoted to requiring terrible engines of destruction which, though to-day regarded as the last word of science, are destined to-morrow to lose all value in consequence of some fresh discovery in the same field. National culture, economic progress, and the producti,m of wealth are either paralysed sr checked in their development. is moreover, in proportion as the armaments of each Power increase, so do they less ane leas fulfil the object which the Governments h. aye set before themselves. The economic crises, due in great part to the system of armaments A outrance, and the continual danger which lies in this massing of war material are transforming the armed peace of our days into a crashing burden which the people have more and more difficulty in bear- ing. Jo appears evident,then, that if this state of things were prolonged it would inevitably lead to the very cataclysm whieh it is desired to avert, and the horrors of which make every thinking being shudder in ad. Vance. " To put an end t 3 these incessant armaments and to seek the means of warding off the calamities which are threatening the whole world, such is the supreme duty which is to-day imposed on all States. Filled with this idea, his Majesty has been pleased to order me to pro- pose to all the Governments whose representatives are accredited to the Imperial Court the meeting of a confer- ence which would have th occupy itself with this grave problem. "This conference would be, by the help of God, a happy presage for the century which is about to open. It would converge in one powerful focus the efforts of all the States which are sincerely seeking to make the great conception of universal peace triumph over the elements of trouble and discord. It would at the same time cement their agreement by a corporate cense:tuition of the principles of equity and right on which rest the security of States and the welfare of peoples."

MAGAZINE CLIPPINGS.

LOVEMAXING IN SLUM-LAND.

The fickle are generally frank in Coster-land, and this peculiarity gives them a charm which compen- sates for other deficiencies. The steadiest youth I ever knew, when nineteen," walked out " with a young machinist. He was one of an o id dozen bun- self,while she was an only daughter. But his mother had come down in the world through marriage, for she had " washed for Government once," which means generally for a detachment of the Police Force, so though she was but a seller of shrimps now,and Joey but a shoeblack, she gave herself airs on the ground of past glories, and was not going to be humbled by Miss Hettie's feather hate. It was a game, however, which Miss Bettie was equally skilled in playing,and before long Joey came to me in a very perturbed state and owned he no longer fancied his girl. " You see, miss," he said, stammer- ing with shame, "it's not fair to be walking her out when I don't mean to have her, but it is very awkward to say I've changed my mind." "And why have you changed it ?" I asked. "It's this way. miss. She wants to show mother she's better off than we, EO she's always showing off. The ladies can manage their own affairs, but she ought to tell the truth to me. She will never go out with me on a Friday night; she pretends she bas visitors. But I know batter: she washes the stairs. Could I be happy, miss, with a wife like that ?" I tried to per- suade him that he was unnecessarily tragic over it, the; Hettie would improve with age, and that, if he had no other fault to find, I thought he was rather hard on the girl. No; he was very unhappy, but his mind was made up; his f aith ha i gone and he wished her to go too; so ultimately he had a stormy interview with the girl, who cried and raged and made a dreadful fuss. For a short time Joey went about looking very pale and sad, and it was some months before he took on with sweetheart N 0.2. She was a very vivacious dam- sel, and for a period completely twisted the shoe- black round her little finger. Then he sheered off and shunned her comp my for no ostensible reason. But she was of a determined disposition and quickly brought him to book. "I'm not going' to be made the laughing stock of th 9 court because of your goin's on, an' so I tell yer plain, Joe Smith. Do yer mean to have me, or do yer not ?" "I can't say that I da jest at present," ha replied politely. "And why not, pray?" she demanded tartly. "Your ways are not my ways," he began mildly, "Then yer might 'aye found it out afore," she interrupted, "and not let a girl waste a fortune in lace i and fallals to look smart for a chap who Can't value good looks when he sees 'em. Now good- bye, Joe Smith! this is the last yer wall see of me, unless maybe yer change yer mind, when 'Liza Dipps is ter be found as usual at No. 7, Stevens Court." With that she bounced off; but he did not follow her then or ever; for though he was curiously fickle, he was curiously firm, and when he found a young lady did not suit him he could not be threatened or cajoled into continuing the acquaint- ance.—From "Love in the Slums," in "The Quiver" for September.

TA RELIC OF BRITAIN'S SLAVE TRADING. A large proportion of the present generation who sing Britons never shall be slaves" will scarcely believe that less than a hundred years ago Liver- pool, now the second city of liberty-loving England, engaged extensively in the trade of carrying human beings as slaves, under the most inhuman condi- tions, from the African Coast to the West Indies and America; and that the city was so dependent on the proceeds of this trade that it was said to be financially built upon it. Such is, however, the ugly fact. For about eighty years tie slave-carry- ing trade was engaged in by Liverpool ship-owners with such vigour that the city was regarded as the chief slaving-town of the Old World. A slave sold for about £4O (prime cost £27, freight and keep £3 155., profit £9 10s.), the net profit on each being about thirty per cent. As late as 1807 Liverpool had 185 vessels engaged in this trade, capable of carry- ing 44,000 slaves. In that year, however, the batt:e in the British Parliament ended in declaring this mode of making money to be illegal. The con- science of the nation had found expression,and was then crystallised into law. The hands that had been " full of blood" were now to be stayed for shame's sake, and for conscience' sake. The accompanying copy of a bill of lading of a Liverpool ship-owner will doubtless interest our readers : "Shipped by the grace of God, in go xl order and well.conditioned, by Irving and Fraser, in and upon the good ship called the Byam, whereof is mister, under God, for this present voyage George Martin, and now riding at anchor in the Rispongo, and by God's grace bound for West Indies. To say two hundred and eight slaves, being marked and numbered as in the margin ; and ale to be delivered in the like good order and well- conditioned, at the aforesaid port of West Indies (the danger of the sea, mortality and insurrection only ex- cepted) unto Order, or their assigns. Freight for the said s'aves paid, vessel belonging to the owners, with primage and average accustomed. In witness whereof the master and purser of the said ship hath affirmed to three bills of lading, all of this tenor and date; one of which bills being accomplished, the other two to stand void; and so God send the good ship to her desired port in safety. Amen.--Dated in Kissing, 14th May, 1803.

'6 (Signed) GEORGE MARTIN."

The average schoolboy will work his fingers to the bone if he has reason to think that a bicycle will crown his efforts (says a writer in." Chums.") By way of experiment, a schoolmaster once promised to give a beautiful machine, to the boy who proved himgelf most worthy of it, and stipu, lated that all who entered the competition should work hard for a whole week, at the end of which time they would be required to submit an account of the way they had spent their spare hours. Ex- citement reigned supreme during the next six days, and many a nome was turned topsy-turvy by zeal- ous competitors for the coveted bike. Every lad chose a different method of showing his ability,and some of the claims handed in were decidedly original. One boy built a substantial workshop' but discovered when he had, finished it that‘he had forgotten to leave a space for the window; another actually made a coffin—" Just your size, sir," he told the master naively; while a third constructed a pretty gingerbread model of the village church, the chances of which were slightly spoilt by the marks of some predltory teeth on the spire. The majority of the lads, however, selected scholastic subjects, and the most maivellous achievement in this line was that of a fifth-form boy, who, without any previous knowledge of shorthand, tack led an old-established stenographic system,and mastered it so thoroughly that at the end of the week he could Write sixty words a minute, and transcribe his notes accuratPly into the bargain. One mu,i- cal genius confidently claimed the bicycle because he had learnt the Jew's-harp, the tie whistle, and the trombone, and was indignant when the schoolmaster passed him over. The prize was eventually awarded to an athletic youngster, who, with inexhaustible patience and skill, taught thirty persons to swim within the time stipulated. The verdict in favour of the last-named i feat was practically unanimous, and the disappoin- ted competitors, gallantly crushing down their feelings, gave the smiling winner a royal ovation. The youngest boy of an aristocratic but needy family used to feel dreadfully ashamed where his chums invited him t o accompany them on their rides, for he hadn't a bicycle of his own, and his father was utterly unable to provide him with one. Thinking that this state of things had existed long enough, he one day resolved to make a bold bid for fortune, and daringly carried his plan into effect It was the beginning of the cricket season, and the boy, having completely hidden his identity by dis- guising his features and wearing well-fitting but shabby clothes, went day after day to a popular county ground and sold penny packets of sweets to the spectators. There was something in his easy, graceful manner and refined accent which attracted general attention to him, and in a very short time it became quite the conventional thing for the ladies in the pavilion to bold converse with the little con- fectionery vendor, who was often given sixpence for a bag of sweets costing only a halfpenny. At this rate matters prospered exceedingly with him, and long before the conclusion of the season he had purchased his bicycle and confessed the deception to his parents. They were very angry with him at firs, esp2cially when be coollystated that be had on several occassions sold sweets_ to his own relatives, saw only the smartness of plan to raise money,andgraciously tbhuetir after 6o u, : rtiismkye , condoned his violation of -the family honour.

_ TEACHING CHILDREN HOUSEKEEPING. In the September number of the Windsor Nap:sine there is an interesting article on the

Domestic Economy Centres, recently opened by the London School Board, for the purpose of teaching girls practical housekeeping. The writer describe" a visit to the Bethnal Green Centre. "The theoret- ic lesson over, the vouni housewives were assem- bled in the bright little kitchen to be assigned their practical work. Two were set to scrub—the envy, these, of their companions; for scrubbing is per- haps next to marketing, the favourite occupation— two to clean knives, one to blacklead the grate and light a fire in the small sitting-room, one to lay the table for dinner, and so oa. Already two had been to market to buy the dinner arid stores for the day, and on their return had cooked tha dinner. Pre-. sumably it was Sunday. or the artisan would not have been treated to such a feast of roast beef, potatoes, French beans, and batter pudding Miss Horner, looking like a hospital sister in her pretty cap an i apron, flitted from room to room, giving hints to the little kitchen-. maids and housemaids, and,'above all, to the young tablemeid, who esideatly thought a carving knife and fork and tablespoons were useless com- modities. It also wanted a gentle hint from Miss Homer to remind the little maid that the angles of the table were not the places that diners would chose to sit at. It is in laying the tables that most mistakes are made,' remarked Miss Homer; and,listening to her instructions.one thought of the debt of gratitude that future employers of these young tablemaids would owe her. At last the table was laid,and the two young cooks were called to dish up the dinner. But the proof of the pudding was yet to CW1216. In the Belgian schools of house- wifery the young cooks eat the dinners that they cook. In Bethnal Green the pudding and its preliminaries are put to a more severe proof, for they serve as lunch for the teachers at the do- meitic economy centre. On the day of my visit,the young cooks scored a triumph with their nicely- browned meat, potatoes boiled to perfection, aid everything else to match."

BY la. WILLIAM ELLIS.

THE duty of England in regard Englan" to the Duty.conference proposed by the CZAR is free from ambiguity. The Government; cannot hold aloof, even if the conference should become a congress. Mistaken and impracticable the idea may be, but it is one that accords very much with an overwhelming body of British opinion, and it would never do to allow it to be said that England was always prat- ing of peace as the greatest of British interests, and was yet unwilling to take part in measures for establishing it on a firmer basis. We are vitally interested in Egypt, and Turkey, and China, and the affairs of all these countries must be discussed as a prelude to any policy of disarmament, or of , arresting the present diversion of international energy and finance to warlike preparations. We can 'honestly say that our military force is purely de- fensive, and not large enough to threaten any 11 Power in Europe. Our naval force is also required to protect our commerce, and our foreign and colonial possessions. Some of the European Powers might suggest that we should cease to have great naval plans every few years, but the reply is obvious, and it ought to be convincing. For the rest, we may have to be very specially concerned. It has struck many persons as singular that just when we were in imminent danger of coming into collision with Russia on matters due to her territorial and commercial aggrandise- ment a project should be broached for averting collision. It may well look as if there were a trick in the whole affair, and that Russia desired to postpone what many persons regard as an inevitable conflict between us until such time as her commerce had enriched her, and her railways were better deve- loped for offensive purposes in the Far East and in Central Asia. But it is believed that the Czar is sincere. He wants to see Russia relieved from the incubus of military preparations and expenditure, and a new direction given to the thoughts and energies of his people. It is a laudable desire, and one with which we have no right to quarrel. Russia is the last of the members of the European system to be under the domin- ion of a military caste and a purely poli- tical autocracy. The commercialising influence was sure to affect her sooner or later, and it is apparently strongly at work at the pre- sent time. The country exports more than it did at any previous period of its history. Railways

have developed her resoursei. Raw materials of varioub birds are produced in abundance. Th° people are not content with a system of agricul- ture which has in it many communistic element 3 The country is certain to become rich and peig'W ful, if internal divisions and revolts do not cut the huge empire into two. A real desire for th° avoidance of ruinous wars ought to meet with 00: warm approval. Hence, whether we think practl. cal good will come of the conference or not, we gr. bound to accept the invitation to it.

Jr hardly appears that ea Have we any New Vet we have begun in thie Century Dreams? country to indulge in asl twentieth-century dreams. We have put on re' cord, in many ways, the achievements of tho cep' tury now drawing to its close. They have been wonderful in all ways—in science, in art, in !it'll' tnre, and in forms of locomotion. We have take* in hand the organisation of municipal life, their.' provement of the conditions of labour, and the betterment of public health. Our free librarl°' our art galleries, our museums, our increased Opel spaces and parks, and our charitable movemee° have marked what may be called the advent of * new spirit. In commerce, we have formed lo'' and general chambers to discuss details and brio% pressure on the various Government departmeats, In education, we have instituted a natio* elementary system; we have establishel continuation schools; we have mad° yearly grants to the support of schools set! their teachers; we have removed the disabilities sl Nonconformists at our Universities, and we hale made a beginning in the foundations of technics and secondary education. Literature has bee* made cheaper, with the result that there are me° Journals, magazines, and books than were knoo in previous centuries. Railways, steamshiPs' telegraphs, cheap fares, and bank holidays hail contributed to travel, recreation and business eIY terprise. It may still be asked whether the national type of character has corresnondinfel improved, and whether we are about to bee s new century with all the advantages of the old one pushing us forward in a healthy and hopeful 015; nor. What are our dreams for the coming center/ It is much to be feared that we have not se dreams of a kind that quicken the pulse and 01; • mine the mind. The great causes that once anima: as have been carried to a completion of some Or Slavery does not exist in the British EraPirso; Missions have been established in most parts the world. There is a greater measure of religiel; and political liberty. But is there a firree; stronger, and bolder belief in human Progress r' We doubt it. Success has deadened effort,prosPeo ity has brought its love of ease, territorial grog 0 has produced restlessness, religious freedom 11', tended towards laxity of conviction. It 01,, almost be said that we have no animativ: dreams in which humanitarianism plays is;of' important part. Progress has become he'd n a bank balance. Education has develce oaf a cynical spirit. Amusements have destroys,. 0, belief in steady self-cultivation in leisure helot We seem to have come to look up to the Stat° es° almost everything. The newest thing is adr 4 of Anglo-Saxon unity. It is abut the only noyel:; we can see. The century is going out and we "40 not made up our minds to anything. The aatio drifts, but it cannot be said to dream. We Mad money, and we are content. It is not a vtieo, satisfactory state of things. The way of is in our men of letters and our divines. 'jot% times we doubt whether either class realises 1 full responsibility.

the small circle of those who are, more or lees, in- terested in the " trade " and who profit by the ' exchange which takes place. And what after all dO the brewers offer? In almost every case it +consists of licenses which they hold by permission of the Justices from year to year, and which the Justices can withdraw if they think fit without way consideration whatever in the nature of a quid pro quo. At the last sessions the magistrates were graciously told that the brewers intended to re- arrange the licensed houses in the town, and another part of this programme was carried out C 55 Thursday.

The Licensing Committee have allowed Mr. Wil- riam Bolton to exploit an entirely new district by erecting a new public-house there. For this great concession Mr. Bolton will give up a fully-licen- sed house and a beerhouse in an area where there are numerous licensed houses, and also an off- licensed house in tho district where the new house is to be erected. That was the bargain struck. The views of people in the Howley district who are to have this new house thrust into their midst re- ceive scant consideration. The process of obtain- I ing a license is now very simple. A brewer has merely to prove that a certain district has in- creased in population, that the inhabitants cannot obtain a drink of his wonderful liquor without walk- ing at least a hundred yards, and having formulated his terms the probability is that the magistrates Will accept them. The result will be that in the 'course of a few years there will not be a single dis- trict without liquor-dispensing establishments at every convenient spot. If such is to be the case the sooner the public are allowed a direct expres- sion of opinion in these matters the better. The brilliant Liberal victory in the Southport division is full of significance. It pronounces in a most emphatic manner an adverse verdict upon the foreign policy and domestic legislation of the Present Administration. It is the straightest hint Lord Salisbury has yet had Vast his foreign policy does not meet with the approval of his countrymen. The issue was clearly placed before the electors, and the par- ticular views of the Government were kept in the forefront. The triumph of Sir Herbert Naylor- Leyland in such an important Lancashire consti- tuency is a blow to the Government and will possibly result in modifying and influencing its policy. Another important element in the con- test has been the actiom of Mr. Holden, a prominent Liberal Unionist, who on this occasion cast in his lot with the Liberals.

_ As an extensive manufacturer of cotton goods he has suffered by Lord Salisbury's Anis-management of foreign affairs in Madagascar, Tunis, Siam, and the Far East. In Madagascar, or instance, since the French were allowed by our "strong foreign minister " to take possession of it qlr. Holden's firm which has six mills has not sent Yard of calico there. It is therefore not surpris- . '4g that he should declare that the basis of his 11, Monism existed no longer, and that he could not oe dragged by an issue obsolete for the moment at ..the heels of a foreign policy he knew to be ruinous 40 the country.

Compulsory vaccination is now a thing of the Past, and the objector, whether "conscientious" or not can have his own way in regard to the vaccina- tion of his children. Such is the result of the new 4-ct, the practical working of which provides a problem which the magistrates make no attempt to active. Now we learn that even the sworn declaration of conscientious objection by the applicant is not obligatory. A. verbal statement by the anti-vaccinator will,in all probability secure !aka the immanity of his children from the lymph Annoculation. A parent may object for any reason that suits him, and not be required to detail the gr, minds of his objection for the information of the 'ench. The provisions of the Vaccination Act sq!`ve provided some puzzling cases, but the magis- :r, ates seem to have found a simple and ready me- ‘riod of meeting the principal difficulty. To all in- tents and purposes the Act might as well have been abolished for vaccination is now optional on the part of the parent, instead of compulsory by law. if the victims of small pox fail to increase in num- ber during the ensuing twelve months, the anti- vaccinators will have an opportunity for swagger- ing which they are not likely to let slip.

A LOCAL REGUiENT TO GO TO PRESTON.

We bear on good authority that it is has been finally decided that the lat South Lancashire Regi- ment is to be stationed at Preston during the ensu- ing week. This will be good news to Warrington and the neighbourhood, for, though recruited here, the battalion has been quartered either in India, the South of England or Ireland since its appsintment to thie county, which has prevented the relatives of these serving in it from meeting their "soldier or getting to know the regiment which is &00ked upon with such pride by those members of it who occasionally get back home during the fur- -1:"Igil season, as "the best regiment in the army." When these fine strapping fellows of a year or two's service are compared with the newly joined teerait at the DepOt,much praise should be given to the organisation which is the means of providing ,412i5 increase of stalwart thews and sinews to this %battle, on their return to civil life. It is pleasant Leo, to see the pride taken by the men in their regi- 'went, which is well warranted by its past history Mtd. present splendid regulation, and we should ongratnlate ourselves on being so well represented an the Army ef England. It is therefore to be hoped that the stay of the itegitzent at Preston will be a pleasant one, bring- . tog es into nearer acquaintance for the future, and eitchi7ls Us to watch its doings hereafter in distant parts of the Empire with pleasure. Given the Flportunity to meet and welcome the regiment in Warrington itself, its reception here would be most niitbusiastic." ' SOUTH WALES COLLIERS AT RUNCORN.

A number of colliers, who have been thrown out of employment in consequence of the Soith Wa,eys coal strike, appeared at S. John's Presbyterian Church, on Sunday evening, and sang several choruses dering the service. The titles of the selections were :—" Like the grass " (in Welsh), " Consider the lilies," and " The Crusaders." The combination forrn an excellent choir, and their vocal efforts were of high merit. There was a large congregation, and at the close of the service a col- lection was taken at the doer in aid of the Colliers' Relief Fund, which realized over £3. MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN, RI:MOORE. On Sunday evening the Rev. W. El. L. O'Rorke, M.A.. one of the chaplains, preached a special sermon at Run- corn Parish Church on behalf of the Mersey Mission to Seamen. Te rev. gentleman referrel at lengch to the valuable work which was being accomp'ished by the society, and mentioned the fact that a new steam launch had recently been purchased, at a cost of £3OO, which absorbed £l6O per annum in maintenance. This launch performed useful service in visiting the lightships, light- houses, and vessels that were wind bound in the channel. An offertory was taken in aid of the mission funds.

- MixTors' WIDNES. A successful gathering has to be chronicled for the open services last Sunday afternoon, when the chair was taken by Mr. George Lacy (one of the vice presidents), who gave an excellent five-minutes' address on the "Three Graces." Mr. R. Roberts, of Runcorn, sang the solo "The Golden Pathway," and he also took part in a quartette with Miss Stone (Runcern)and Messrs. Thomas and Wilkinson, each part being rendered very elf actively and well received. The Rev. Joshua Pedlar, of Tram- mere, gave a very interesting and instructive address on the lamp-post sermon. "Keep to the Right," and he dealt with the subject in an excellent manner, evincing much humour, yet sound advice in his remarks. The meeting was brought to a close with the Benediction.

RAILWAY FATALITY NEAR WIDNES A shocking accident occurred on the Cheshire Lines Railway at Broadheath Bridge, near Widnes on Tuesday afternoon, resulting in the death of Mr. Thomas Davey, aged forty-nine, m %der upholsterar.residing at Islington Liverpool. Atomic half past one,front some unexplained cause, deceased was on the railway, when be was knocked down and run over by a passing coal train. The driver, who did not see the deceased until it was too late, reported the accident te some workmen at Hough Green Station, and they went up the line and found deceased lying face downwards. He was still alive, but had sustained terrible injuries, one of his legs being cut off, his arm and ribs broken, ani his head badly injured. A vehicle was quickly procured, and the unfortunate man conveyed to the Widnes Accident Hospital, where he was attended by Dr. Hutchinson. Everything possible was done for Davey, but he died shortly after three o'clock.

FRODgiia.M.

RUDDIIN Drenr.—On Monday, Mr. James Fogg,Main- street, Frodshatn, died under very distressing circum- stances. It appears that he got up about his usual time and having partaken of his breakfast went into the garden for a walk, returning into the shop and pro- ceeded with his usual work, he being a shoemaker. Shortly afterwards his wife went to call him but was shocked to discover him lying face downwards dead.On Tuesday, an inquest was held on the body before Mr. J. C. Bates, county coroner at which the doctor stated that it was his opinion that death resulted from pressure on the heart caused by deceased falling on his stomach. It was also his opinion that had someone been present to turn the deceased over in time the calamity would have been averted. A verdict of death from natural causes was returned. A RUNAWAY HORSE AT RaODSZA.M.—On Wednesday, a horse and shandry belonging to Mr. John Norcross, farmer, of Kingsley, were standing in the station yard of the London and North-Western Railway SPation, when suddenly for some reason or other the horse took fright, and bolted down Station-road, dashing into the window of the Albert Ins, then turning it continued its flight down Church-strltet, and rooted up two of the beautiful trees erected In honour of the jubilee. Even tually it was captured,little the worse for its caper.

SAD DROWNING CASE AT SANKEY BRIDGES. Mr. Jones, deputy-coroner, held an inquiry at the Borough Police Station on Saturday afternoon into the death of Richard Jenkins, six years of age, who lived at 9, Wright-street, Sankey Bridges, and who was drowned in the St. Helens Canal on Thurs- day afternoon.—Sarah Jenkins said the deceased was her son. He cams to his dinner on Thursday and at ten minutes to one he went back to school. Be was brought home dead by the police.—Thomas Keating, a little boy, said that instead of going to school he and deceased went to play on the canal side at Sankey Bridges.. Jenkins slipped in and witness went and informed a policeman of the occurrence.—P.C. Wood said he was in plain clothes and off duty, in Liverpool-road at about three o'clock on Thursday afternoon. The last witness came Up to him and said "Oh Bobby little Jenkins has fallen into the cut." On being questioned the lad said they were going to school, but instead went to the canal side; Witness dragged the canal and re- covered the body, which was lifeless, aboat ten yards from the bridge on the main road.—By a Juryman: It was a dangerous place, .and. there was nothing to prevent children from falling in. No one seemed to have seen them on the canal side.—Tue jary recorded a verdict of" Accidental death."

Numbers of the residents at Higher Run- corn were employed at Weston and Weston Point, and it took them about 18 minutes to walk up and about 15 minutes to walk dawn, and about ten minutes for them to send to the nearest place for a glass of beer, the whole time being almost gone,and it was a great hardship for them. That they were entitled te their glass est beer, if they wanted it, no one would deny, and as they could not afford to buy it its dozens or barrels, he con- sidered the request for a little aecornmodatioa a very reasonable one. A circular letter was teaeived teem Oil Local Govern- ment Board, adverting to the Vaccination Act, 1898, stating thac the Act would nct coma into force in its entirety until January is , 1899. and calling attention to Section 2, which came into iorce with the passing of the Act. T.e section read "No parent or other per‘on shall be liable to any penalty under Section 29 or S.ction 30 of the Vaccination Act of 1867 if within 4. months from the birth of the child he satisfies two justices or a stipen- diary ar metropolitan police magistrate in petty Fe sons that he conscientiously believe:4 that vacc.nation would be prejudicial to the health of the child, and w tun seven dais theraaftf r delivers to the vaccination offic.r for the district a certificate by such justices or magis- trates of such conscientious objection ; but in its Appli- cation to a child barn before the passing of the Act,there shall be substituted tor the period of four months Inns the birth of the child, the period of four months fr-m the passing of this Act." DISTRESSING DEATH OF SERGEANT WILLIAM ALFRED PLAYER, RUNCORN.

A sad occurrence took place in Greenway-read, Run- corn, shortly after eight o'eleck on Friday night, which cast a gloom over the whole of the district,in the awfully sudden death of Colour-Sergeant William Alfred Player, who dropped on his back in Greenway-road,ane expired in a short time afterwards from heart disease. Only a few minutes before the sad oceurreoce, Sergeant Player had been in the Volunteer Social Club, and, but for a complaint of severe heartburn, appeared in remarkably good spirits, considering that he tiaa not been enjoying his usual good health ter a few days previously. The proprietor of the Lion Hotel (Mr. Thomas Price) assisted uy others carried Sergeant Player into the hotel, but he expired before Dr.Ornesby,who was sent for immediately, arrived. The doctor on arrival stated that the probable Cause of deatu was heart disease. Much sympathy Is expressed for the widow in the sad bereavement, as Sergeant Player, who is only 58 years of age,was widely knewn, and highly respected in the neighbourhood. Born at Erith, in Kent, where his father was organist at the parish church for many years, young Player enlis- ted into the 22nd Regiment, now known as the Cheshire Regiment in 1858. He was then only seventeen years of age, but his education and smartnees caused him to be promoted to lance-corperal within six months of his joining, and he was made sergeant in about three years wade serving in Vialta, and afterwards eolour-sergeano. From 1859 to 1865 Sergeant Player was at Malta. He was subsequently two years in the Mauritius, and was iii one home service from 1867 b 1876; tie was never ii taloa, bum he pess-ssed the good c ieduet medel In the last-named year he was apeointed drill instructer tn the Runcorn companies of the 7ch Cheshire, and 2ni V.B. Cheshire Regiment (Earl ot Chester's Rifles), and three years ago alter a meritorious serviae of twenty yeas in the Volunteers, and ato al service of nearly iorty years he retired on a pension. The occasion was signalised by the preseneaidon to nun of an illuminated address and a valuable silver tei sesvice. He hes since occupied the position of secretary to the Volunteer Social Llub.
