TUE FUNERAL.

The mortal remains of ex-Sergeant William Player, were, on Wednesday laid to rest, in a new grave in 1 Greenway-read Cemetery, Runcorn, with fitting mlitary honours and respectful tenderness, by lo,s late comrades in arms of the 2nd Battalion of the Eirt Cheshire Re.:i- ment. The ceremony, which was an imposing one, was witnessed by so large a concourse of people that it was considered expedient to close the gates of the cemetery. whilst the ceremony was going on. At about 2.30 thel Runcorn Detachment, numbering about 88, exclusive of officers, proceeded from the armoury to toe house of thel deceased, f rots whence they proceeded in the followine order to the cemetery :—Firing party (20) commanded by Colonel Sergeant Anderson ; band (24) paying "Dead I March " in Saul; buglers (4) ; toe coffi 1 and bearers (12); friends; rank and file of the Runcorn volunteers, com- manded by Captain G F Ashton and Lieutenant Maurice, who brought up the rear.—At the church and graveside the burial service was impressively conducted by the Rev. W Scholes (curate). The coffin bore the fot.o wing inscription:—" William Player, died Angus: 27ch, aged 58 years." It was of oak with brass mountings, and was borne from the house to the graveside on the shoulders of his respectful comrades, being covered with the Union Jack on which were laid sword, belt and helmet. Those present included—Captain G F Ashton (command- ing the Runcorn Det schment), Lieutenant. Maurice, Sergeant-Major J Thomas (Chester); Sergeant-Instructor Burke (Hartford), and T Rolley, 2nd V. B. FATALITY IN THE SHIP CANAL. THE BODY RECOVERED.—INQUEST. THE CORONER ON THE SITUATION. Mr. J. E. Warsley, at the Runcorn Police Station, on Wednesday morning, held an inquest on the body of a boy, James Ed warl Owen, woo was drowaed under distressing circumstances whilst bathing in the Ship Canal on Thursday week, and whose body was recovered on Monday last, near the spot at which it went down, after being disturbed by the pasting of the steam boat " Salford."—The father of the bay was present, and Captain Heasley represented the Ship Canal Company. Mr. Richard Owen, 21, Sth Bank Terrace, Runcorn, said the deceased was his 12 year old lion. 'rhe last occasion upoa which he saw him alive was on Sunday week. Personally he knew nothing about the occurrence but his wife had t eld him that James went out of the house on raursday latt week quite well. He was aware that hit boy o3casionally weat to b ithe in the canal down by Ferry hut, and also that he could not swim. No one ever prevented his lad bathing there to his Knowledge though his mother often used to try and distuase him from going there. Witness had seen numbers of boys bathing regularly there, and in fact it was a recognised regular bathing place. For the 20 years he had resided in Runcorn the foreshore had beta se used, especially bet,ra the advent of the Ship Canal. Bertie Lambert, son of John Lambert, 7, Clarence. terrace, deposed to being in company with the deceased en Thursday week, when they weat for a bathe in the Ship Canal, at a spot between the Ferry and Rancor's. Bridge, near to Ferry Hut. Deceased could not swim but he (witnets) could swim just a little. At first they bathed alongside the landing stage, but James said it was not deep enough so they went against the cop, where the deceased must have got out of his depth. Witness swam after him and got dragged down, but deceased let loose of him and sank himself after coming up once. He then shouted and the ferryman rowed to the spot and tried to get the deceased out, hut he waS too late. Witness was accustomed to bathe there, although he had been driven away by the ferryman. They were not always driven away,and when there were one or two of them the ferryman let them bathe. Some- times both boys and girls were bathing there.—By the jury : He learnt to swim by the landing stage, and did not know of anywhere else where buys of tae town bathed.

Mr. Richard Asspey, ferryman, stated that the boys were bathing, unknown to him, on the ThumJay, and must have been by thernselve 4 as he sew no one else there. Whilst he was tying up the boat, and had a pin and rope in his hand he heard the last witness shouting and immediately let go of the rope aad pin and got out the boat to go to oie ass stance. He reached toe apot in a very few minutes afterwards but could not see anything of the deceased. He aitempted to get at the boy with a boat back, and tried for him where the puddle was but could Teich no further teen about 12 to 14 feet. He could sa irn a little but it would have been useless to enter the water as he could not see the lad. ! Whilst he was in the best the Rev. Mr. Stables (of !Weston), cams running from the church and entered the ' water about kaee deep. He afterwards took him in the beat, and they crossed the caoal t 3 fetete the grapples which were on the other side. On coming back they at ! once grappled for the b edy but could not find it. He did not know whether the curate mild swim ; neither could he tell whether the body would stiy where it diseppeered or go with the tides. It was low water at the time of the ac- cident. Several people had been down in the afternoon diving and trying t) recover the body. At the close of his examination,witaess stated from time tu time he had warned the children about bathing there, aud it was a great pity that the parents of the children did not take more care. He had beau sitting, on many oceasions, in his boat long enough to get sunstroke, keeping guard over the children—warning them back lest any of them should get drowned. He was net; :surprise i at the occur- rence. The only thing that surprised him was that there were no more such accideots.—By the j Jry : He could not say how many he hid. seen bathing there at a time. He had been continually warning them —One of the jury,remarked that be tied seen 80 whom be counted himself,batbing at the spot. Mr. James Percivaesinitary inspector to the Runcorn Urban District Council, said he recovered the body from the Slip Canal on Monday evening. The body waa re- moved to Owen's home. There were no marks of violence upon it, but there were several traces of the grapples having go; on to the body. It was takes out in close proximity to where the boy was said to have sunk, but how it was it remained there,was beyond him,especislly in the face of the numerous attempts to recover it. In summing up, the Coroner said, it was a very sad inque.t—it was an inquest of great importance, and an inquest he had foreseen, and the probability of which had often been commented upon by ferernen of previ- ous juries. They could see from the evidence of the boatman, that he too had foreseen the result, when he stated that he often stayed in his boat in danger of getting sunstroke simply for the purpose of watching over these little children. He had warned them and even driven them away, and it was still his opinion that if they did not go away these deaths would be repeated. At length, Mr.Woraley detailed the listery of the plane, which he described as being of a peculiar nature. The depth from lor 2 inches at the eittreese water edge, came gradually to depths of 2 and 3 fee',then suddenly without any warning it fell toe depth of the whole 24 feet of the canal. There being nothing to mark the change was it not likely that the little children would meet their deaths? He had had the opportunity of visiting the spot, and was pi epsred to make any presentment the jury thought fit to require of him either to the Ship Canal Company, or to the Local Authority. in the shape of recommending the erecting of a rail sufficiently high and strong to be a raw* and protection against danger, and cutting off , the piece of water like so much impounded specs, to provide a safe bathing place for the children. Boys would !bathe, and during the hot weather nothing would prevent then* from bathing. As to the actual verdict he could only recommend them to bring in one of "Accidetital Death" as there was no one to blame. Indeed, it was apparent to all, that those who bathed there were trespassers up. on the Ship Canal property. After a brief retirement the jury returned a verdict of "Accidental Death." On behalf of the jury, the fore- man proposed a vote of condolence with the parents Of the boy, which was unanimously accorded; and asked that the Coroner would make a preseatrnent ay suggested to the Ship Canal Comp any with a view of protesting the piece, failing whicn they would like the Local Authority to be approeched.---Captaic Heasley said he , would lay the matter before the Ship Canal Company. \

A RUNCORN BOY DROWNED.—TRASIC TERMINA- TION OF A SOLIDLY. Joseph Williamson. the fourteen-year-old son of Am- brose Williamson, of Clarence-terrace, Runcorn having a holiday,decided to spend it in what to a juvenile mind would be a highly romantic way—on his uncle's- boat on the Bridgewater Canil. He joined the boat on Friday night, and all went <veil until Sunda, night. The boy had greatly enjoyed himself in visiting Alexandra Park. Manchester. in the afternoon, and after tea want for a stroll with his uncle. Just b3fore ten p.m. they returned to the cabin,and were proDatinX to go to bed when de- ceased,who had taken off his coat and shoes,leEt the cabin and went on deck. Be was shortly afterwards missed, but nothing was seen or heard of him again until day- break on Monday morning,whan bia body was discovered just under the stern of the boat. The canal was dragged for more than two hours the yrevious night. An inquest was held on the body by the City Coroar -on Monday. Acting on the presumption that the boy had walked off the deck into the water the jary returned a verdict of Accidentally drowned." THE PENERALS. -

In these days of keen business competition, and brisk commercial enterprise when the markets are flooded with various kinds of articles of food and drink, and when people arematurallY desirous of getting the best value for their money, it is only reasonable to suppose, that there will be a "survival of the fittest," and that only those products which possess sterling merit will long continue in public favour. Amongst these there is one_which has won its way to the front by sheer , merit, and merit alone, and that is Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa. This wonderful Food-beverage has become known 'even in the most remote districts, and as the knowledge of its excellence has increased, so also has the demand.

A Rural Postman, (Mr. R. Parry, 12, John Street, Winsford) writes desire to add this =solicited testimony to the sterling qualities of Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa. I am a rural postman am exposed to all kinds of weather. I have used Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa daily this last eleven months, and I believe I can do my work with less fatigue since I have used it. I can honestly recommend to my fellow-postmen Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa for its sustaining qualities."

THE TRAGIC SUICIDE IT ORFORD BA IMAMS.

Last Thursday under very sad circumstances. Sergeant Instructor William Healey of the 3rd South Lincashire Regiment committed suicide at Orford Barracks. He was known to have been suf- fering very acutely for some time past from neural- gic pains in the head, and had been intending to go ' into the hospital for treatment. On Thursday after- -1 noon ho had arranged a cricket match, Barracks v. Police, but the Barracks did not raise a team as Sergeant Healy was not about. Nothing much was thought of this, but towards nine o'clock in the evening his lifeless body was found lying in a pool of blood in the musketry room in the Barracks. Further examination showed that he had placed his foot through a loon of cord which he attached to the trigger of a and with his mouth over or close to the muzzle had discharged the firearm. The bullet had passed right through his head, and en- tered the ceiling of the room. The unfortunate soldier wss one of the most popular men in the barracks, and was the moving, spitit in all cricket, football,and athletic matters. The decea-ed was ex- tremely well-known not only amongst the military men in Orford Barracks, but with the Warrington public. He was secretary of the Lancashire Tours- day Half-Holiday League, and a prominent mem- berand efficient referee of the Warrington and District Football Association. His twenty•one years' service would have expired next spring, when be would have retired on a pension.He was about forty years of age. He is survived by his widow, for whom much sympathy is expressed on every hand. INQUEST AND VERDICT.

At the Police Station, Warrington, on Saturday. Mr. Jones, deputy-coroner, held an inquest on the body of Sergeant Drill Instructor William Healy, of the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Orford Barracks, whose death we recorded last week. Frances Eh aly said the deceased was her husband, and they lived in " E Block," at Orford Barracks. He was 38 years of age. At a qnarter ti two o'clock on Thurs- day afternoon he went cut of the house to the stores to work. She asked him not to go, because he told her he was Buffering severely with pain in his head and face. He told witness be would go and finish his work, and would see the doctor the next morning. She had not seen him since. He had suffered for a long time with his head and face, which was caused by having a tooth wrongly drawn about two years ago. He had under- gone tw operations. He was very depressed, and said, he was worse than ever on Thursday, and he could not eat any dinner. He hod disehareel blood from his core in the morning. There was no came: why he should take his life eeelet the severe pain he suffered. Provost Sergeant Morgan Henry Kelly said he saw Sergeant-Instructor Healy at 2-45 on Thurrday after- noon in the sergeants' mess. He told Men he was suffer- ing from severe pains in the head. He went out of the mess and die sot say where he was going. Witness met deceased's wife at 8-35 the same evening, and at her request he went to look for Healy. He went to the armoury room and found deceased lying on the floor dead, with his mouth open. There was blood about the place. He did not examine him, but went to repert the matter to his superior officer. He also saw a rifle lying across &ceased's feet. Witness said that Healy was en good terms with everyone in the barracks. Colour-Sergeant Welsh slid he saw Healy at four minutes to thre e on Thursday afternoon in the Armoury Stores. He told witness that he was nearly mad with his face and head. He had complained about it since Tuesday. Healy left him, saying he was going to the shirt-room. Witness did not see him alive again. Police-Inspector Taylor said the matter was reported to him at 9.30 p.m. on Thursday. He at once proceeied there,and was taken to the Armoury, where he saw de- ceased lying on his back in a pool of bleod. On calm n• jog the body he found the chin was black with powder, and hie mouth was open. There was a bullet mark in the roof of his mouth and one at the back of kis head. From the marks of blood he came to the conclusion that Healy had been standing upend shot himself. Witness scorched the place, and in the ceiling, zorresponding to where Healy had been standing,he found a spent bullet buried four inches. The distance to the ceiling would be about four feet from Healy when he was standing up. There I was a rifle !ling at his feet,aed a piece of cord attached to the trigger. There were two loops at the loose end lof the strneeand the assumption was that Healy put his foot in the !roes of the string to pull the trigger. There were two cartridges lying by him. In deceased's pocket there were sew ret letters in connection with athletic matters. On Thursday afternoon he had arranged a cricket maech between a team from the Barracks and the Police, but the former team was not raised.—By a juryman : Nobody heard the report of the gun. lospector Spinks said that Healy was a general favour- ' Rein the town. Captain Hawes spoke of the high esteem in which Healy was held in the regiment. He performed his duties consclentiously, and officers aed men were extremely ' sorry to hear of his death. The jury recorded a verdict of " Suicide whilst of un- sound mind,caused by intense pain." Tney desired to extend their deep sympathy with the widow. Mrs. Healy thanked the jury for their expression of Ilsympathy. THE FUNERAL.

Oa Monday sfternoon the remlins of the deceased were interred with military honours in the Warrington Cemetery. THE IMPENDING DEPARTURE OF THE WARRINGTON POSTMASTER.

INTERESTING PRESENTATIONS. Mr. Adlard presided at a meeting of Post-office officials held on Saturday in the Sorting Office for the purpose of presenting Mr. West on his promotion to the Postmasters ship of Sunderland with a handsome smoker's cabinet and outfit. The cabinet was euitaely inscribed. Mrs. West was also made the recipient of a prettily-designed diamond brooch. , The CHAIRMAN, in calling upon Mr. Brest%Unit, the chief clerk, ta make the presentation, refereed to the ex- ce lent character Mr. West haa established during his short stay in Warrington, and expressed. regret at the set( ranee which the promotion involved, at the same ' time cmgratutating him on his advancement. Mr. BROADHURST, in making the presentation, spoke very highly of Mr. West, both officially and personally, characteriz3ng him as an official who had been unbiassed, conscientious, zealous, and faithful in carrying out his duties, whilst personally he had set thipen a lasting ex- ample of courteous consideration in all his dealings with the staff and the public generally. Mr. BENNETT, supurintendent, cordially supported what bad been said, and congratulated Mr. West on his prametion to Sunderland. Be wished Mr. and Mrs. West and family a happy future. Speeches were also made by Messrs. Janes and Banks, of the clerical staff and by Miss Richards, the official in charge of :he Market-placa Post Office. Mr. Mellor, inspector of postmen. testified to the high esteem in which Mr. West was held by the postmen, and Inspector Emu bare similar testimony on behalf of the telegraph messengers. Postman J. Skelton having spoken for the postmen and Mr. Wright, J.P., sub-postmaster of Church-street , for the postmasters of the district, Mr. wren replied, expressing his grateful thanks to the staff on behalf of Mrs. West and himself for the handsome and useful gilt; presented to them. The gifts would, be said, be treasured as mementoes of their happy soj turn in Warrington, and of the kind and very agreeable relations which had exist'd b.tween thorn and the general staff of the Warriogton Postal District. Mr. West humorously remarked upon th 3 association of the young and the old Who had presented him with a smoker's outfit. Be did not think it desirable that ' young Persons.shoulci indulge in smoking, and his habit shoold not be regarded by them as an example. If they did follow his example, it would be te abstain till they were 40 years of • aeis„ for it was that age at which h. commenced to smoke. He eulogized the character for steadiness, z sal, and efficiency. of the staff,and repeated what be had always maintained, that a better staff dii not exist in the service. He trus'ed, however, that this knowledge would not render them toe self-reliant, but would have the effect of stimulating their character for aeensacy in order that it might always be theirs. He referred to the happy stay he had made in Warrington. Be would ever remember the place With -sincere affec- tion, because of the many acts of courtesy and kindness received from all its citizens with whom he had come In cootact.

A vote of thanks to the committee, proposed by Mr. Brladhurst, seconded by Mr. Bennett; also to the Chair. man. proposrd by Mr. West. and reconded by Mr. Read4t, and suitably responded to by Mr. Melia' and Mr. Adlard, brought the proceedings to a close.

In consequence of famine at Mosul. and the vicinity, the Armeirans are suffering terribly, and many Christians are stated to have sold their daughters to obtain bread. The last sitting of the Iron and Steel Institute was held s.;, Stockholm on Saturday. Zing Oscar entertained the dele- gates and other rnests at'stapper at Drottningholm Castle. PRESENTATION TO MR. GEORGIC WLLDE. During an interval in the entertainment, Councillor Dr. Cannel!, who presided said be must in the first place apologise for the colour of the water in the bath, which had been caused by the storm though it was perfsctly clear when seen in a glass, and was none the worse for swimming in. He had been requested to present to Mr. Georze Wilde, of Legh-street, a medal from •tae War- rington Sa,imming Club for assisting In saving a boy from drowning in the canal on Walking Day, Mr. Wilde he said, was not a perfectiswimmer, but he had risked his life in endeavouring be save that of another. Refer- ring to the value of swimming, the speaker said the Swimming Club considered it their duty to spread a kaowledge of it, not only among men and women, but , among those of the younger generation: (Applause). They had all read of the terrible catastrophes which had taken place during the last two or three months, and they had read in the account of the yacht accident which took place recently off the Isle of Man how a young lady was able to swim five or six hundred yards to save herself, whilst her sweetheart and everyone else were drowned. That showed haw important it was that everyone should know how to swim. I He wished them to understand that those galas were not organized for the benefit of the Swimming Club; they were not promoted fram mercenary motives ; they were held simply with the object of teaching everyone the value of a knowledge of swimming. Speaking Of the presentation, the doctor observed that the C 193 occurred on Walking Day, when Wilde plunged into the canal without divesting himself of his clothing, and with the assistance of a boatman rescued a boy who was drown- ing. They did not give Mr. Wilde the medal for its intrinsic value, but simply as a token of their apprecia- tion of what he had done, and thy hoped in that light heiwould value it. The value of the medal was slight, butlit was the value of the life that tie medal represen- ted he had saved, which they must look upon. (Loud applause.) The doctor then presented the modal. Mr. Wilde expressed his thanks to the Swimming Club.

SCIENTIFIC LADY SWIMMER. The neat event on the programme was one of con- siderable interest, particularly to the lady members of the audience. Miss T. Conway, ex-amateur champion of Manchester, gave a exhibition of scientilc and orna- mental swimming, which in no way belied It.r reputa- tion, her pOrforma-na being received with hearty applause.

WATER POLO.—NORTHERN COUNTIES CHAMPIONSHIP. SEMI-PINAL. Osborne met Newton Heath in the semi-final of the N,rthern Counties Chamnionship in water polo, and of course this constitutod the most important item on the Programme. The teams were as follows :—Oaloorne : G Flarnrott. goal ; T Coe (Inter.), T Harria (Int,r.), full backs • W El Liater (Inter.), half back ; J Robinson, J H Derbyshire (Inter.), P }1 Lister, forwards. Newton Heath; W Hathway. goal; 9 Key, El Dean (Lane. Conn. 1896), backs; S Dean, half back ; J Hayes, W Kay (L.C., N. of E.), T Hodson, fermards. The Osborne team had held the championship for five sears until last year, when they were defeated by Everton. This year they have met end defeated Everton. Osborne defended the deep end, Newton Heath enjoying early advantages in the game but did not score as their 'shooting was ill-judged. Good play by Coe changed the course of the game,and a pass from P.H.Lister to Derby- shire remlted in the champion scoring with a splendid shot which quite beat the Newton Heath custodian. The same tactics by Osborne resulted in another goal being added to the score by Derbyshire. Newton Heath for a time changed the venue of the play in their favour, but could not score when they had the opportunity, though much was owing to the ashrrne goalkeeper. No change in the score was made, half time coming with the result Osborne two goals to nil. The changed position on the resumption of the game should have been of some advantage to the losing team, and far a time they tried their oppenent's defence very much, but it was not for long. P.C. Dister passed to Coe, who shot from a considerable distance, adding another score. A capital combination by Newton Heath was successful, despite the efforts of Osborne, Haves ecoring their first goal. Just before the whistle was sounded, Derbyshire shot in, the ball was thrown out by Hattaway, but again returned, this time successfully. The final soore stood —Osborne, four goals ; Newton Heath, one goal. COMIC RACE. This event caused considerable amusement, as the competitors were obliged to swim two lengths of the baths in nicht-dress and to carry a lighted candle. The effo.t required a great deal of manipulation and skill to keep the light irt. The race proved a good one. W. Creighton, securing Ist prize, tea pet ; and A. Baker, end prize, match box.

THE NUISANCE OF THE NIGHT SOIL COLLECTING VANS.

Sir,—! am now up aking of certain streets in upper Howley in the neighbourhood of the inter- cepting depot. At the end of the week, C3(11- inencing at about half-past ten at night when the workingmen have retired to rest, and have to rise Surly in the marling, they cannot go to steno through these heavy four wheeled collecting vans rolling through the streets, shaking the whole of the houses as they pass, and the men in charge sometimes shouting at the top of their voices. Cannot something be done to relieve the neighbourhood of this great nuisance? I my- self would rather put up a ith tha little smell and have the pails collected as they used to be in the day time, rather than this disturbance at night. Faiiing t have it altered, cannot the wheels be so constructed (tyred if practicable) so as to lesion the noise ; and the men cau- tioned not to snout to one another. Ido not want to harass the Corporation in any way, but believe rue sir, these vase going about at eight are an evil—a sore one,—and a remsdy if possible /mould be found. Workingmen who have to toil day by day must be protected.—Yours truly, 15 and 17, Welliegton Street, Warrington.

S. DAVIES.

THE BREWSTER .SHSSIONS. Sir,—The action of your magistrates on Thursday last in granting new licenses is simply appalling. When all over the country we find magistrates curtailing drink- ing facilities and giving every encouragement to those who are striving to diminish the " temptations to drink " here in Warrington our magistrates support the trade at the expense of the public. I sho Id like to know what our Christian churches in Warrington are doing to arouse thur congregations from the apathy which undoubtedly exists in the town. Where are our ministers? With the exception of about two of the youoger Noncooformi,t ministers all the rest are absolutely silent upon all questions affecting the well-beitig of the community. I also protest against Corporation officials being al- lowed to support these " drink" applications. It is Simply scandalous aed a disgrace to the town. I hope the Temperance Feleration will bring pressure to bear upon our town councillors and thus prevent our officials being dragged at the heels of tsse beer barrel. AN OBSERVER.

Dear sir,—Oae or two olsservstioas seem callel for with respect to the recent Brewster Sessions in War-

rington. 1. The Limosing Committee and the Magisterial Bench should remember that they possess only dele- gated power from the supreme power in the country, and that government is meant to be carried on with a view to the wen-being and not in the direction of the moral degradation of the people. There is the highest authority for saying that magistrates and other rulers bear the sword of justice not as an object of terror to good works but to the evil ; it follows, therefore, that in an mattsrs of licensing the moral welfare of the people Should be the first consideration. 2. It is generally allowed that every public house is a source of degradation to the surrounding, community, hence the necessity for those extraordinary safeguards which the law imposes in connection with all such places, which legal securities nevertheless fail to prevent many of the evils attendant upon the sale of intoxicating liquors. It should, therefore, be the policy of every bench of magistrates to keep within the smallest possible: limits the facilities for the sale of these dangerous beverages. So far from this being toe case in Warring- ton there is one public house to every 294 inhabitants. It is contonded by many social reformers that one public house to, every thousand of the population is as many as ' should be allowed under pr. sent circumstances. Cer- tainly such a state of things would inevitably lead to a , groat iraprovetnent in the social and moral condition of the people. S. The system of bargaining with brewers for the giving up of licenses in old and congested districts in lieu of licenses for public houses in new and developing localities, is one which is fraught with great evils to the communities in the midat of which these new houses are placed. It simply means the extension of ths pre- sent distressing condition of things, for in every manu- facturing town the tendency is for the centre to become more and more the business part, and as a consequence the people are driven to the outskirts for homes. To plant now licensed houses in these new and growing neighbourhoods is as highly detrimental to the inhabi- tants of such localities , as the old licensed houses were to the former inhabitants of the now sparsely-populated centre of the town. 4. That public officials of the town should give evi- dence in favour of the granting of licenses is a preresding Of which the strongest disapproval oust be expressed. This year the market inspector gave ovidence,last year the Town Clerk did so, and two years ago another. official, in a court outside the borough, supported an application in favour of the granting of a license for a new hotel quite near to the Sanitary works.As the ques- tion of licensing is one upon which the community is divided in opinion public officials, should, at least, observe an impartial attitude, or if they exhibit any bias at all it should be in the direction of the moral Wellbeing of the people, and not in the opposite direction 5. Why is it that with one or two honourable excep- tions the clergy of the mother church in Warrington take no a lequate steps to grapple with the great evil of intemperance from which Warrington suffers so much? ' Is it because the gifts of the brewers prove a powerful impediment to that freedom of speech and energetic action, which are so necessary on the part of leaders in the Christian church if the people of this country are to be delivered from what the late Duke of Albany declared was the only terrible enemy England had to fear, viz : Drink ? 6. The most cursory observer must admit that the condition of Warrington from the drink evil is simply appalling. The statistics of the Chief Constable are a very poor criterion as to the real state of the case. The number of drunken people to be seen in the streets any Saturday is saddening in this extreme while the prosecu- tions of publicans for permitting drunkenness are almost The ramifications of the liquor traffic penetrate into every part of the life of the people. They penetrate into the church, into the Council Chamber, and into the magisterial bench. They penetrate into the athletio exercises of our young manhood and it is not to much to say that the life of our nation is b sing poisoned at its source by the increasing drunkenness amongst young men and young women. The present Condition of things will continue to exist until the Christian Church and Temperance Reformers shall become So united, and so strong in organisation, that they will no longer toter- ate an evil which lies at the root of nearly all the crim 3, pauperism and insanity and general degradation which exist in our country. May God hasten the time when this shall be the case! Yours faithfully, 17, Museum-street. EDWARD T. REED.

BA.PTISM AL REGENERATION.

Sir,—Admitting himself beaten on the ridiculous question of "Baptismal Regeneration," "Sure= Corda" now turns his attention to Protestants and the Vible. In doing so he does but exhibit his ignorance, and once again shows that he knows no more of the Scriptures than suits his purpose. It nosy be news to him that Bible reading is enjoined by the scriptures themselves, and that right from the earliest times parents were directed to personally instruct their children in the Law of God. The Saviour himself exhorted the Jews not merely to read but to search the Scriptures, and we learn that the Bersans " were more noble than those in Theasalonica, in that" (after a visit from the Apostles taut and Silas) they received the Word with all readi- ness of mind,and searched the Scriptures daily, whether those things were so." Now, sir, it seems to me that alter this example I need go no further, as it completely disposes of "Sursum Cor laVargument in favour cf teaching only by priest;. Really " Sursiorn Gorda'" argument appears quite childish as I shall endeavour to show. Biblical students are well aware that the Bible hos come to us through the Jews who coastantly met it in the services of the syna- gogue, and whea Jesus visited the synagogue and took part in the s3rvices, he " Begin- ning at .Moses and all the prophets, expounded not them in all the scriptures the things concerning himself?: Thus what Christ did himself it is permissible for his disciples to do. To the Jews all was plain sailing. They had the scriptures, read them, and could follow in them the references to the Messiah. But the Jews, re- jected Christ, and then He directed his disciples to go to the Gentiles, who knew nothing of the scriptures—hence the command to ~gaye,theref ore, and teach all nations." It was only teachlng that ceuld reach these people, as to-day i it s only teaching that can first of all reach the heathen. But with the invention of the printing press, educational methods have been greatly simplified and assisted. The Bible has been translated into the vulgar tongue (iii spite of the Catholic Church), and has been disseminated amongst the people (in spite of the Catholic Church. ) who are now able to aid and test the teachers by reading for themselves, and seeing, lik3 the Bereans, whether they are taught aright. But " Sursurn Cords ' would have us put the clock back, and go hack to the era before printiog.when the scriptures were kept from the people, as amongst the Remanisti to-day. What a lot of kno.w- ledge would he imparted to our youth if their instruction was wholly of an eral character, and they were rigidly kept from home lessons and books. Sursum C )rda " prides himself on the " trua unity" of the Catholic Church, and I was reminded last week how beautifully it is maintained. A paragraph went the rounds of the papers stating that at Licht, a coMbrated ausicia;who wee giving ""ezbi in aid of We poor, was

hailed to prison at the instance of the parish priest, be. I cause he refused to bow to the blasphemous host in the public streets, he being a Protestant ! Have we not reason to be thankful that we live in a Protestant country? The unity that Christ would have es strive after aad obtain is the unity of love, and not the unity of coercion. Truth is many side', and no one church— not even the Roman Catholic Church—possesses a mono- poly of it. So long as men differ in their mental capa- city, and temperament so long will there be difference' of opinion on religious matters,and so long will there be scope for the exercise of the Christian virtues, mail all who exhibit the possession of thole virtues are members of Christ's church. It ie easy to see that these people have " one Lord "--Jesue Christ ; " one faith "—belief in Him "one baptism "—th it of the Holy Spirit. The real church of Christ is not an earthly institution, am " &triune Cords" would have us believe,but a spiritual church, and all who humbly follow in the footsteps of Jemus Christ, and ceek to do Hie w.ll, are members of it. In this Church toere is the fullest unity, and the widest liberty. To it entrance is obtained not by rites and ceremonies, but by lowliness of heart and simplicity of life, which can be attained by reading the Bible and following its precepts. In the pages of the Bible we find all we re- quire tor out salvation, and thus it includes all finality. We real that we have an advocate with God in the person of his son Jesus Christ, and therefore priests saints, virgin, aad pope are altogether useless as ap- proaches to the Almighty. The Bible alone is the Chris- tian's guide, and I trust I have plainly demonstrated, that we have every warrant for reading it,—Yours !cc., A BIBLE CEIRLSTIAN.

BAPTISMAL REGENERATION AND AUTHOR' r Y

OF THE BIBLE.

Sir, —Your correspondent " Somme Cords" has in several of his letters referred to the " baptists" in such a way as to indicate that he could not argue with them on the subject of Baptismal RegeneraVon,simply because they took their stand upon the " canon of Scripture." In this matter he shows wonderful d scretion and I must congratulate him. Hs evidently aceep s the testimony of Cardinal Manning, who in discussing this question with the Rev.W.Werd, Callege,Cambridge —a clergyman of the Church of Eoglaad—said " You must know, sir, that every learned mao, who has exam- ined for himself, bath in your communion and in every other,has been forced to concede the point to the Bap tists." And Cardinal Manning must have the credit of having made one Baptist at least, for in four months afterwards this clergyman entered the B iptist ministry. Doubtless the Baptism have a strong position, for they stand upon the "impregnable rock of Holy Scripture. They will not accept the decision of the Path •r, nor the decrees of councils, nor the authority of churches in Rome or Canterbury unless they will stand the test of the Word of God. In his letter of the 27th :tilt your correspondent takee an attieude towards two of the most cherished beliefs of Baptists and Protestants, whieh in the estimation of Baptists and in the light of scripture is as utterly inde- fensible as that which he tak is on Baptismal Regener tion. Take his attitude te oard the Bible. He labours te prove that the supreme authority in religion is riot the Bible—but the Church. nest, by asserting that the Church was before the Bible, or as he ingeniously puts it before the canon of scripture. Now this is only partly true, for the greater part of scripture : the Law, the Nelms, and the Prophets, tegether with meat, if not all the historical !mks, were read, studied, preached from and sanctioned by our Lord, and his Apostles, long be- fore there was a Church. This partially false statement vitiates the whole of that part of his argument. Secondly : By attempting to show that certain practices mentioned in tee New Testament, are not observed arid I others again adopted by Pretatants—on the authority of the Church, thus giving countenance to his idea that the Church is a higher authority than the Bible. This we I deny, and assert that of the pracciem he mentions—with I the exception of Infant Baptism which is non-existent in the Bible—there is suffieienti evidence in the Bible it- self, that they were of a local, and temporary character, and not intended to be of universal or perpetual obliga- tion. , There is one thing which your correspendent has not deemed it wise or prudent to mention, viz., that there is a considerable difference between the early Christisn Church and the present R man Catholic Church. Up to the third century there would probably be but little cause for diversion in our ranks as Christians. When however in the third century Infant Baptism crepe in, there began te weave around it and out of it a net work of error, in which the Rornish communion has been more entangled than any other, and welch requires on their part the actual prohibition of the Bible, which they claim, their own Church canonised in order to cover their corruption. Lt me give a few instances. Cardinal Bellarrnine, a great oracle of the Roman Church, writes :—" We seeintain that the Scriptures ought not to be r sad pub Liv in the vulgar tongue, nor allowed to be read iedifferently by everybody.' The following is f eund under the fourth role of the prohibited books—ordered by the Council of Treat and approved by Plus : "If anyone witheut ii. license pre- sume to read or keep by him the Btole, he shall be dis- qualified to receive the absalution of his sins till he , deliver it up to the ordinary." It Is to be heped that your corresplecient has secured this license ! Foley a license required to read the Bible, ye liberty-loving Britons ! just as thugs it was some nitro-glycerine you had to handle ! and yet the canon of this very book is claimed byl the Catholie Ceurch ! What was the Church about woen it gave such a dan- gerous thing to the world ? That it is a dangerous thing let Dr. Cahill testify, for he declared—aecerding to the " Tablet" of December 17th, 1853, p. 804—" Ra would rather a Roman Catholic shoeld read the worst works of immorality, than the Protestant Bible—that forgery of God's word, that slander of Christ." Again—we have Pius VII, in 1818, issuing his bull against Bible societim, which he describes as " a crafty device, by which the very foundations of religion are mad ermined, a pesti- lence which must be remedied and abolished, a defile- ment of the faith,eminently dangerous to souls, impious machinations of innovators, wickedness of a nefarious scheme, snares prepared for men's everlasting ruin." All this is owing to the existence of the Bible, end tint " Sursum Carda " assures us was given to us by the , Catholic Church. How is it that they hive such a ter- rible opinion of their own gift, which they nevertheless regard, as your correspondent does, as a Revelatioe, of God inspired and divine? Let Cardinal Wiseman tell us. Referring to the seeeseions from the c lurch of Rome he testifies "The history in every single case is simply this ! Teat the individual by some chance or othet. probably through the ministry of some pious poem, became possessed of the word of God, of the Bio'e ; that he perused the book. That he could not find in it tran- substantiation, or Auricalar confession ! that he could not find in it one word of purgatory, or worshipping of images. He goes te the Priest and tele; him that he can- not find these doctrines in the B.ble. His Priest argues with him, and endeavours to convince him that he should shut the book that is leading bins astray :—Ele ' perseveres, he abandons the communien of the Chu.ce of Rome, and becomes a protestant." I After • reading this we cannot be surprised at the hatred which Roman Cithiliess manifest to wards the word of God. They are afraid of it I and well ' they may be for it is irresistible and as it circu- lates through the wield the errors and superstitions of IRome will be vanquished, and the power of Popery be , shattered. Nor is it any wonder that " Saran ii Cards', ' who only reads the Bible by permission, aad who dared :net be present at any religious service outside his owe I community—should be so grossly ignorant as to suppose that we regard the Bible as essential to salvation. Our ministers teach and preach,our missionaries do the earns, and the only things they regard as esserriel to salvation are repeatance towards God and faith in tee Lord Jesus Christ. " EVERY PASTOR IS CALLED OF GOD"

Sir,—The following remarkable statement appeared In the" Manchester Guardian " of the 15th inst and is attributed to the present President of the Wesleyan Con- ference. "In the Christian Ceurch wit must remember we must no overlook the fact that every Pastor is called of God." Tails utterance is far too grave to be allowed to pass unchallenged, an I I, therefore, tea t you will kinely allow ins a portion of your correspondence columns ie which to apply Some amicable tests to it. The phrase, " Christian Ceurch " at once suggests the very important question, where is that institution to be found ? Answers coma from many quarters at once. Some boldly assert that it is the Churca of Pater. of which the Roman Pontiff is head : others again firmly deny this, and contend that it is the State Religion over wnich Clasen Victoria preside; ; while our Nonconformist friends of all shades put forward a similar claim for their respective Zions. lat once con- fess my inability to adjudicate upon these rival claims. But I may be permitted to express my opinion that the differences both in doctrine and ritual whin divide the claimants are rather airainst than for any or all of them being regarded as the Christian Church. Gad is certainly not the author ef the confusion which prevails among them ; and surely the religious babel in wince we live cannot be cousidered as an answer t the Saviour's prayer, that his disciples in all ages might be on, even as he and his Father are one. (John xvii. 11 to 21). Passing from the undecided question, where is the Church to be fouud, I have now to deal with the snit more questionable proposition of the Pastors' calling. Mr. Hughes tells us, apparently without any doubt sr hesitation, " that every Pastor is called of God." I may say at once that my faith is not sufficiently strong to accept this dicenn in the sense obviously impl Iby the President. True it is indeed, and right gladly do I own it, that every pastor who knows, by happy experience what it is to be a Christian, that is a follower of Christ —has indeed been called of Gad. For did not JeSl.ll say to the Jews, "No man can come to Me except the Father which bath seat 119 draw him ; and I will raise him me at the last day." (John VI. 44). But this drawing or callieg is not confined to the ministerial class, but is common to all believers; and its fruits are ; seen in a godly and ceusistent life. Such a life, how- ever, does not entitle any man learned or unlearned. poor, or rich, to lord it over his fellows, or take credit for gifts and graces which he does not possess. Then there is another—a special calling of God, and I believe this is the one to which Mr. Hughes refers. The nature and effects of this special calling will be best illustrated by examples. In Old Testiment times Moses and Aaron were reised up and called in a very remarkable manner for a very remarkable purpose; and ISO were Elijah, Elisha,and the other Prophets. And we learn from the Scriptures that all these men were em- powered to show such signs and wonders ' hat their corremporariss must have seen that the haul of God was with them, and that they were indeed, what they claimed to be, the appointed s vents and messengers of the le erd of Hosts. Ner was this divine calling less signally manifested in the primitive days of the Church of Christ. The Apostles were very bumble men, drawn in fact from the ranks of labour ; but so marvellously were they fitted far the mission to which the Lord had called them that they maid preach in unkaown tongues, discern spirits, confer the gilt of the Holy Ghost, and perform miracles on the bodies of men. If the Wesleyan President asks me to believe that be and his fellow pes- ters were raise lup ad called to the ministry after the manner of the Prophet; and Apostles then indeed he makes a draft upon my credulity which I cannot honour. Aim why? Because, look and march where I will, I cannot find a scrap of evi lance to support such a belief. %fedora pastors, so far as I know, are most re- spectable snen,fairly educated, and desirous, in most in- stances,to leave the world bstt than they found it : but of the kens which distinguished the Lord's special servants oi old they are,one and all,abselutely destitute. lain bound to admit, however, that there is a calling , with which all pastors, including the President himself, must be credited; but unfortunately for thernet is of the earth, earthy, ael not of heaven. Every pester who occupies a pulpit nad to find his way to that position through an entrance of some kind. In the church of which Mr. Hugh Price Hughes is a star of the first in lenituie the way lies throueti the college and the cm. franc 3. But surely no sensib e man, not even Mr. Hughes himself, will expect me to regard these ecclesi- astical doers as divine institutions! The college exists for the disseminttioa of secul sr and sectarian knowledge; and th 3 Conference is neither mere nor less than an assembly of highly respectable but on- inspired meneweli versed in matters pertaining to their profession ; but without any divine authority or power to confer spiritual gilts urn(' their fellows. But even this earthly cosliee,po or as it undoubtedly is,is not with- out attractions for many minds. In lesd it is very popu- lar and maces :ought after in these days fur reasons whichef not very cenarnandable, are at least intelligible enough. True College and the Conference, apparently Without any fear of being charged with impiety, center upon their elect the high honour of prefixing their names wee one of the attributes of God ! Then again they prescribe peculiar garments by whice their disciples may be seen and kno ern of all men. And lastly, when they have title .I and dressed them to their liking they send out their el ct t preach the gospll for a good salary. All these things may he very gratifying to the good pastors and well, o•do p‘sople who follow atter them ; but by no stretch of irnivaatisa can I look up ea them as signs of a divine mein. Oa the contrary,they appear to me to be purely human devices to secure popularity and proit to persons who, if they wers really whet they re- present tennweves to be, would be the humblest and most self-denying of men.—Yours truly,

At Sed,gley on Wednesday evening the South Staffordshire Coroner opened an inquest concerning the death of a pupil teacher named Hilda Meredith. The girl • had passed one examination and was studying to pass a second. Owing to not being able to complete her lessons, she became depresset and said she would drown herself rather than go on teaching. Subsequently she was foiand dead in a pool a considerable distance from her home. The Coroner said that as her tongue protruded from her mouth he did not think that death was caused by drowning. There were, indeed, no symptoms of drowning. He adjourned the enquiry for a post-inortem examination to be made.

There loss been great drought at the Antipode----s, bet the Australasian " Review of Reviews "announces a copious fall of ran over the whole country, and says :—" The parched Inland plains will soon be "covered with lush green grass, leafy orchards, and the yellowing corn. How cruel in intensity, how costly in results, the long-sustained drought has been is hardly realised. Sir William Zeal, at the annual meeting of Goldsbrough, Mort, and Co.. Limited, held in Melbourne on Jane 29, tried to express in arithmetic the cost of tbe drought. The loss in wheat, hay, oats, &c.. to Victoria during the three year. of drought he reckoned at over £5,000,:10J ; the loss in wool, Ice., for the same period was at least another £5.003,000. Thus the three years' drought cost Victor,'" alone something like £10,000,000. The "Investor's Review," discussing the question "Is there tobe cheap wheat says :—The English and Conti- nental supplies may be taken as pr,tty certain to be consider- ably in excess of those of last year. Prices will consequently rule lower, and we shall not be so much dependent on the American supply. This is the view now taken by cautious observers in the United States, but the Western farmers are still holding back. Supplies of new wheat are put, but spar- ingly, on the markets. The present caution has reduced the " visiole supra " below all past records, but then it is known that many times that quantity is " almost within a day's run of Chicago." In a week or two probably, when a more trustworthy estimate can be formed of the probable European and other supplies, the American farmers will become more free in their sales, and wheat will settle down to something like its natural price. Whatthat price may be no one would venture yet to suggest with any confidence. It may—pro- bably will—go Lelow even last year's average. It is interest- ing to note that 1697 was the first rear since 1892 that the average price f wheat had gone much above 26s.per quarter. If the average price for wheat In this country during the latter half of this y.ar goes as high as 30s. we shall be much surprised. It is more likely to be 21a, and It may fall even below that.

CYCLING. Cycling wag of course out of the question on Saturday owing to the rain. A few of tbe members of the War- rington Cycling Club took advantage of the fins weather oa Tuesday night to have a run to Newton. To-day the members have an inter-club run with the Manchester wheelers to Daveaham.

Arthur Adelbert Chase is once again the record hero of the hour by reason of his magnificent ride last Mon- day—next ta Betts' mile of 1 m'n 35 secs the finest record performance of the year made upon an English track. Chase was the first Eaglish rider to cover over 30 miles iii th-• hour ;he was also the first to ride over 33 miles. Ideal weather for record breaking prevailed last Monday night, when at about half-past six Chase, io his usual brown and grey costume,which so well matches his dark complexion and skin, turned out for an attack on what is reckoned, next to the mile,the " thickest" of re:lords. Everything was in order for a grand hour's burst, bets being made previously that Stocks' long-standing re- c)rd of 32 miles 1,086 yards would get a mile's beating some even considering Tayloure's world's records in jeepardy. Chase rode a preliminary lap, and looked tighly fit. He rode 120 gear and 26in. front wheel. From the " five " Stock's records were mowed down with persisent regularity. Chase rode gamely, and had the pacing been faster could have followed it. Tay- bum's times, it was evident, would not be equalled, but a loud cheer went up when Stocks' famous distance was ' eclipsed. Chase finished well, and received a rousing , cheer. The principal new English times were : One Imile, 1 nain 52 25 secs ' • five miles, 8 min 47 1-5 sec ; ten 17 min 36 sec ; fifteen miles. 26 min 33 3-5 sec ; twenty miles, 35 min 44 sec; twentrfive miles, 44 min 46 4-5 sec; thirty miles, 53 min 52 2-5 sec. One hour, '33 miles 712 yards, Tayloure's hour record was one mile faster.

The question now arises, Will English riders and pacing be able to regain the rec ords annexed in America? Chase had everything in his favour, he is now at bis fittest, and the pacers were never riding faster, yet he finished some way outside the American times. Several say that under the present pacing conditions it is im- possible for Chase to improve on the ride to the extent to best world's records. It must be considered the cracks in tic States have undue advantage,as they have twice as much pacing on hand. The latest particulars of Edward Tayloure's mile ride of 1 min 32 3-5 sec at Philadelphia state that 15 of the fastest rid srs on the 1 pace-making team of the American Association wore placed on quints to pace him. The start was a faultless one, and from the very shot of the pistol the wee French- man flew at a terrific pace directly at the rear wheel of the big multiplets' which thundered round the board course. He went the first third in 30 2-5 sec, the second third in 31 1-3 sec, and the last in 31 sec, making a total of 1 min 32 3-5 sec. When the time was announced the little champion got a rousing reception.

The increasing number of accidents remin is us that "He who rides with cars away may live to ride another day."

R. L. Jeff.3r3ol ,acc-iinpanied by camels carrying water and teat bearers, is now cro33iog the Kizil-Kurn desert.

Platt-Betti is slowly reelvering From the effects of the serioas accident he sustained a short time ago. Be has hopes of being able to race again next year.

FOUND DROWNED. On Monday afternoon, Mr. F. A. Janes, deputy coroner, held an inquest at St. Helens Town Hall, on the body of Michael Murphy, 52 years of age, of Raven- t, who was bund drowned in the canal near to the werka of Messrs. Quirk, Barton, and Co., where he was employed. It was stated that on Tuesday night Murphy, left tie works to fetch some beer, and would have to pass alongside the canal. He did not return, and it is suppo,ted that he fell into the water and was drowned. A verdict cf " Found drowned " was returned.

EARLESTOWN AND DISTRICT CYCLE.

PARADE.

The second annual cycle parade, fanzy these car- nival and torch light procession took place on Saturday under very unfavourable cireumstancss. The parade was held under the auspices of the Earlestown Social Cycling club, and great prepara- tions had been made to ensure a successful show. The weather,however, which is an important factor in all outdoor displays of this kind, proved a dis- turbing element, and Saturday morning opened in a most depressing manner,and considerably damped the expectations of the committee who had charge of the affair. Rain fell heavily all the morning,and 11 although there were signs of tan improvement at noon, there was another deluge which continued until after two o'clock, the time at which the pro- cessionists should have mustered. Ira the mean- time the committee met to consider the state of affairs, and anxiously debated the desirability of postponement. Towards three o'clock, the rain ceased and the sun shone brilliantly, and it was thereupon decided to assemble at four o'clock, and this decision was quickly circulated through the town. At four o'clock there was a very large mus- ter of cyclists on the Market Plac' e but just before making a move, another heavy shower of rain de- acended,which caused a scamper. In a few minutes it cleared off and from this time onward the waather continued beautifully fine. The marshals were Messrs. J. Preston, G. Wright, W.MawdsleY, J. Smith, J. Jones, W. Gilmour M. Andrews, J. Arm- strong, T. Topping, T. Holliday, J. Gregory, and J. Williams, and they quickly set about putting the medley into something like order, in preparation for inspection by the judges Mr. F. T. Parry, Liverpool and Mr. Charles E. Pugh, L.D.C.C.A. Mr. .1. W. Thorpe should have acted also, but he wa, prevented by illness from doing so. Attsr a short delay, the procession moved off headed by about a dozen of the Lancashire Yeomanry in command of Sergeant Swans, and an imposing dis- play they made. The following was the order of the procession which followed :—Earleatown Prize Band, tricycles and tandems, lady characters (no-, comic), gentleman characters (not comic), juveniles—boys and , girls, Professor Coolies' Wellington Rand, gentleman comic characters, lady comic characters, club groups, chums, Parr Bt. Peter's Prize Band, lady's best decor- ated machine, gentleman's beat decorated machine, neatest individual (lady),neatest individual(gentleman), club groups, comic characters and novelties, Runcorn Blue Hungry Band.

The route traversed was as follows :—Market-squ irfti Market-street, Noith Sankey-street, R,gentstreec, Via duct-stroet, Wood-street. Bank-street Earle-streetiQueer" street,Wargrave-road, Park-road, Willow-road, Mill-lane, (turn at" Millstone "), Church-street, High-atreet,Crow- lane, Cross-lane, Bridge-street, Market-street, Ground. Along the line of procession large numbers of people assembled and evinced the greatest interest in Market the decorations of the bicycles and the costumes of the cyclists,which were pronounced to be excellent by ever!- body. The procession was not quite as numerous as it would have been had the weather been tine, but as the judges remarked "it was far ahead of many others they had judged, in point of excallence,the decorations being at once unique and rich." A new feature introduced into the parade this year, was that of Collectors wise were all attired in some costume characteristic of some- ' thing,and doubtless this contributed in no small measure to the success of the parade. One character, amongst the collectors stood out in bold relief, as Wing most ex- cellent and that was" Lily White," a young fellow from Runcorn, whose delineation of a female was beyond compare. Another excellent character was that of an acrobat undertaken by a gentleman from Liverpool, which called forth admiration.

The evening promssion did not leave the Ilarket ground before half-past eight, and at this time all the main thoroughfares were simply impassable, the town being crowded with people. There were several addi- tions to the parade at night, and the machines were beautifully illuminated with fairy and chines° lanterns, whilst additional light was added by means of torches, all of which tended t.) lend brilliancy to an already brilliant scene. Toe order was samething as before, but the route was considerably shortened, as follows : Market-square' Legh-street, Viaduct-street, Earle-street, Queen-street, Railway-street, Houghton-street, Darby- street, Hotel-street, Cross-lane, Bridge-streot,Markst-st., Market groued. On returning to the Market ground, there was a fireworks display,which was witnessed by a surging crowd of people. The following is a list of prize winners in both par- ades :

OHM Y'S CI RCIJS.

_ Good audiences have been attracted during the week to this place of entertainment by the fine pro- gramme submitted. Gartland, the clown, continues to provoke laughter and amusement with his jokes and tricks. Miss Meyers with her troupe of per- forming dogs and her horseback riding, still proves a draw. Claude Ohtny, gives a varied exhibition of riding and together with Patterson, some fine combination tricks in their line are provided. The Brothers Zoltine are very successful and earn warm applause. Watson and Toney are still popular in their riding feats; Little Toney, however, puts a finish to the business with his dashing performance, which brings him unlimited applause from the audience. The wire performance by Fere is an attraction which has enduring .charms. The com- pany has been greatly strengthened by the musical Ho wards in an original entertainment, and Mons. Thorne, the ladder King, with some risky feats on the ladder which are well received. The last, but not the least important Ifeature,is the Kinetograph, by which some excellent and new pictures are thrown on the screen. The entertainment is quite up to its usual standard.

Hints on Cooking Corn Flour. Notwithstanding the wide use of Corn Flour, particularly in summer, as a cool and refreshing Blanc-mange, many fail to bring out its true and delicate flavour. To thoroughly enjoy a Blanc-mange : FIRST.—Make wholly with good sweet milk without any addition of water, and do not use too much Corn Flour. ozs of BROWN & POLSON'S "PATENT" Corn Flour to two riles milk is the right proportion.) SECOND.—BIend the Corn Flour with a little of the milk, bring the rest to the boil, take off the fire, and add the Corn Flour slowly. THIRD.—Then boil well, in fact, boil down to three-fourths of the original bulk, which will take about ten minutes' boiling. To use warm, turn out when cold, and reheat in the oven. FOURTH,—And above all, use only and always the recognised best— BROWN & POLSON'S "PATENT" CORN FLOUR. It is more delicate in flavour and goes further than others. One pound of Brown & Poison's Corn Flour will make twelve one-pint puddings. Thus the cost of the Corn Flour in a pudding is so small, that everyone can afford to have the best. Ask for BROWN & POLSON'S CORN FLOUR —'`PATENT" is their BEST QUALITY—and be sure that you get it.
